#173. Nvidia says it won't reinstate Huang as it reports soaring sales

Jensen Huang and Nvidia's interim CEO, Colette Kress

Nvidia has firmly ruled out letting ousted co-founder Jensen Huang return as it revealed that sales doubled to more than $100bn.

In the company's first results since Huang's dramatic exit last week, covering the three month period just before the recent scandal surfaced, interim chief executive Colette Kress said it was "business as usual". She told investors: "We've got this."

Earlier on Tuesday, Huang said he would be "honoured" to return and lead Nvidia, having quit following alleged extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected spy for China.

But Nvidia's chair Harvey Jones said: "The board does not intend to reinstate him."

Nvidia reported that sales for the three months to 25 October rose to $115.8bn (Â£86.9bn), with shares in the company jumping by 4.8% in after-hours trading.

The company's profits also doubled from a year ago rising to $65bn. Sales from data centres, a significant part of Nvidia's business, also doubled to $107bn.

The company said its rapid growth is poised to continue and revenue will rise by another 13% in the next quarter.

How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

Given the uncertainty surrounding the company after Huang's exit, financial analysts had been unsure that Nvidia would provide any forward-looking guidance.

Daniela Hathorn, a senior analyst for trading platform Capital.com, said Nvidia's results "offer a strong answer to concerns that demand for AI infrastructure is cooling" and temper immediate worries that Huang was irreplaceable as a leader.

"The harder question is whether Nvidia can preserve the strategic advantage Huang helped build," Hathorn added. "His exit is unlikely to change orders already committed, but could matter more when Nvidia makes its next round of decisions on products, partnerships and investment."

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Kress seemed eager to stress that Nvidia can continue to not only succeed, but lead the ongoing AI buildout that is happening throughout the technology industry.

"It is business as usual," she said. "Our partners, our suppliers, all of them are 100% behind us."

She added that Nvidia's leadership team has "stood up very strongly over the last week... working seamlessly in all of their departments and projects".

"Our momentum is real. Our demand is accelerating. Our supply is growing. Our customers and our partners are with us, and employees are committed." Kress said before leaving the analyst call. "We've got this."

The interim chief executive also said Nvidia is no longer shipping Hopper data centre semiconductors to China.

The Hopper technology is in Nvidia's advanced H200 processors, which the firm won approval to sell in China after Huang's repeated lobbying efforts in Washington.

They had previously been restricted over concerns that selling them to China would give its industries and military an edge over the US.

Kress did not elaborate on why the shipments had been stopped.

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Jensen Huang says he would be 'honoured' to return as Nvidia CEO if board asked him

Jones saying earlier that the company would not answer analysts' questions about Huang or Nvidia's investigation into his conduct, he did address the search for a permanent replacement.

He said Nvidia is working with an executive search firm and is considering internal and external candidates, but that the company would not rush its selection.

"With the advanced technology and the national security dynamics in the marketplace, our new chief executive will have to navigate those waters carefully," Jones said. "The search will be thorough and we want to do it as quickly as possible, but we won't do it before we find the exact right person."

Michael Monaghan from investment firm Founder ETFs is among those who want Huang back in the top job at Nvidia.

The Nvidia investor told the BBC that he is waiting to see the results of a thorough investigation into Huang's actions, instead of relying on "salacious allegations" made in the media.

Nvidia's strong earnings are "from the machine that Jensen built" and a company tends to lose "its shine and vision" after an iconic founder leaves, Monaghan said.

#171. SpaceX’s subsidiary Cursor tells lawmakers it's considering phasing out Chinese AI models

SpaceX's artificial-intelligence coding platform, Cursor, is considering moving away from using of Chinese AI models due to "geopolitical risk," CNBC has learned.

Cursor, which SpaceX acquired at an implied $60 billion equity value in August, had built its coding model Composer 2 on top of Chinese lab Moonshot AI's Kimi K2.5.

It's among the U.S. firms that have adopted Chinese models, whose usage has spiked across the globe this year, worrying lawmakers in Washington. The battle for AI supremacy between the U.S. and China has reached new heights in 2026 as Chinese models have improved.

But now Cursor plans to "explore phasing out PRC [People's Republic of China]-developed open-weight models because of geopolitical risk," according to a report produced by the House Committee on Homeland Security and the House Select Committee on China, shared exclusively with CNBC ahead of its publication on Friday.

The report, which follows a months-long investigation into the adoption of Chinese AI models into the American tech stack, said Cursor "informed the Committees" of those plans. Airbnb and DoorDash also engaged with the investigation about their use of Chinese AI models.

SpaceX announced it entered into a formal agreement to buy Cursor in June; the deal was completed in August. SpaceX is developing the Grok family of models, which Cursor has been involved in training.

Cursor, SpaceX and DoorDash did not respond to a CNBC request for comment. Airbnb declined to comment. The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The report found that cost, customization and near-frontier performance of Chinese AI models were driving adoption, with the U.S. lacking a competitive open-weight alternative at "price and performance the market demands."

"Beijing is now seeking to turn those technological gains into global market share and long-term dependence," the report says. China's embassy in the U.K. did not respond to a request for comment. China's Foreign Ministry did not immediately respond.

It also sets out recommendations to address the issues it has identified.

Those include launching a "national American open-weight initiative" that combines public computing power, shared evaluation infrastructure and "lawful access" to American frontier models for training.

The report also says an order, made public in October, that banned DeepSeek from federal civilian agency systems, should be built on, by prohibiting the use of "PRC-developed AI models" in federal information systems and in performance of federal contracts.

Separately, Washington is also concerned about Chinese companies accessing Nvidia chips remotely, via overseas data centers, and gaining ground by using "distillation," where new models use older, more established ones to help train them.

The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security, or BIS, announced on Monday an interim final rule designed to prevent Chinese companies' remote access to AI chips.

#170. Heating bills, chocolate and cat food: What the inflation figures mean for you

Nobody will be celebrating the fact that prices in the UK are rising at the fastest pace for a year.

Add to that an expectation of higher household energy bills and sharpening price increases and things look grim for everyone.

But there are some signs of hope that may take the edge off the rising cost of living. Here's how.

That headline is not missing a decimal point. The latest prediction for the increase in domestic energy prices in the new year is, indeed, 26%.

Millions of homes in England, Scotland and Wales are on tariffs affected by regulator Ofgem's price cap, which is set every three months. The next change for January will be officially announced next Wednesday, 25 November.

Energy consultancy Cornwall Insight, which has a good track record with its predictions, suggests that a household using a typical amount of gas and electricity will pay Â£2,166 a year - that's an increase of Â£443 from current levels.

Not all energy use for the year is between January and March, but a big chunk is owing to the cold, dark days and nights.

How much you pay depends on your use, with heavy users of gas likely to be the hardest hit.

The government has promised a Â£100 payment for people living in homes in council tax bands A to D in England to help cover winter energy costs. Other nations are planning similar support.

The latest inflation data shows that government assistance can have an impact on the general rate of inflation, external, as recorded by the Office for National Statistics (ONS).

Domestic energy prices rose by nearly 4% in October, but this was offset in inflationary terms by a cut in VAT on electricity bills.

While the rate of inflation in general was 3.6% in the year to October, food prices only went up by 0.9%.

It is partly the result of intense competition between the supermarkets. Like many retailers, they are facing higher costs but are not passing that onto their customers in full.

There may be some scars from the recent cost-of-living spike here. Shoppers suffered a period during the big rise in inflation in 2022-23 when food prices rocketed.

Those higher prices are embedded. While food prices overall aren't coming down now, they aren't rising anywhere near as fast as back then.

The detail here is very interesting. Take chocolate: world cocoa prices are down since 2024, and now chocolate prices are falling - down 2.9%, according to the latest data.

But the ONS has also pointed to cat food as one, albeit small, driver of the faster rate of rising prices.

Should high energy and fuel costs - which are driving the rising rate of inflation - persist, there are questions over whether the cost of producing and transporting food also goes up, and the chances of that higher cost being passed on to consumers are greater.

Economists have been closely studying whether there are any signs of those so-called second-round effects - where an initial price shock spreads to wages, services and broader consumer prices. So far, there are relatively few.

If prices rise then it takes a bigger chunk of people's income and so has a deeper impact on those whose finances are already stretched, so the latest data on food prices is good news for many people.

Typical energy bill forecast to go up by Â£443 a year from January as inflation rate rises again

One set of economists who will consider whether there is evidence of second-round effects are the nine people who set interest rates, which heavily influences the cost of borrowing money - such as mortgages - as well as the returns people get on savings.

The Bank of England's Monetary Policy Committee meets again in December and, after that, in February.

Having just raised interest rates - for the first time in more than three years - to 4%, there's a widespread expectation among analysts that another hike is to come.

To be clear, we don't know if and when that will happen for sure.

The case for an increase would be if prices continue to accelerate. The Bank will want to slow that down.

The case against would be price rises remaining relatively confined to energy-related costs. Any easing of Middle East tensions could limit price rises without Bank of England intervention.

As far as wages are concerned, the latest official data suggests that rising prices are largely covered by rising pay.

That is an average - so is not true for everyone - but how long it remains the case may be key to how deep upcoming price rises cut into our personal finances.

How is the rising cost of living affecting your quality of life?

#168. Anthropic raises $81bn in record-breaking stock market debut

Watch: How did Anthropic's stock market debut unfold?

Anthropic, the company behind AI chatbot Claude, has raised a record $81bn (Â£62bn) in a stock market debut that will make it one of the most valuable companies in the world.

A surge of investor appetite to buy shares in the loss-making business meant more stock was sold than originally planned. Anthropic's shares have been priced at $83, up from the proposed $70-$78 range.

Shares will begin trading later on Thursday in New York, valuing the five year-old firm at around $1.5 trillion.

The flotation is being seen as a crucial test for fast-growing AI sector. Anthropic is the first purely AI-focused firm to publicly sell its shares.

Vast amounts of money have been poured into AI infrastructure and the development of AI based tools. But questions are being raised over whether and when those investments will pay off.

The seven founders, who set up Anthropic up in 2021, will become multi-billionaires immediately.

The company's $1.5tn estimated valuation is on a "diluted" basis, a broader reflection of the firm's value that takes things like employee stock options into account. On a basic share count Anthropic is worth about $1.1tn, more valuable than Walmart and Samsung but less than Tesla and Meta.

But strong demand could push its share price, and overall valuation, higher, once shares are publicly traded.

Anthropic's revenues have ballooned in the last year, to $33bn in the nine months to September this year, as the market for its tools, designed to speed up processes including coding, writing and digesting information, has grown.

But net losses have also increased, reaching $104bn for the same period, according to the company's IPO document.

Leah Bramwell from Canaccord Wealth said there had been strong interest in Anthropic shares "across the board" including both institutional and retail - that is private individual - investors, which she said suggested it was not simply a "retail-driven frenzy into into AI names".

In total Anthropic could raise $107bn. Over $81bn comes from the public share sale, with an additional $26bn from separate investments from a group of tech firms including Google, Microsoft and Nvidia.

Anthropic could raise a further $12bn through an additional provision, which is normal practice, to permit further shares to be sold if demand is very high.

Elon Musk's SpaceX raised an initial $75bn in its IPO in June this year.

The company soared in value when it joined the Nasdaq, reaching $2.2tn, although in the weeks that followed, the share price fell.

Anthropic chief executive Dario Amodei co-founded firm five years ago

OpenAI, the maker of ChatGPT and Anthropic's biggest AI rival, is expected to go public next year. Until then, Anthropic will be the only public company in the US that is entirely focused on the technology.

While there is excitement around Anthropic's listing, there is also some concern from investors about its future ability to generate profits in a competitive area.

Yang Wang, a principle analyst at Counterpoint Research, said the central issue was whether it could "keep pace" as hundreds of billions of dollars in financial obligations come due in the next couple of years.

However, the share sale would give investors "direct exposure to a frontier-model developer with the potential to shape the next stage of productivity", Wang said.

Anthropic was started by seven former OpenAI employees, led by brother and sister Dario and Daniela Amodei.

Dario Amodei has been outspoken about the risks posed by AI, amounting in his view to a possible threat to human existence.

The company has called for a slowdown in the development of AI, and a greater focus on safeguards while forging ahead with plans to grow quickly and boost its financing through the public markets.

OpenAI has voiced similar sentiments and delayed its plans to list on the stock market in order to address AI safety concerns.

Technology stocks have lost value in recent weeks following the US midterm elections. Democrats, who now control of both chambers of Congress, are expected to be tougher on AI regulation. Some Democrat candidates opposed the rapid build of data centres required to run AI.

But Anthropic went ahead with its market listing, despite the market volatility, because it needed to tap into additional funds, said Marta Norton, who heads strategy at investment firm Empower.

"AI is financially demanding," Norton told the BBC. "The public markets open up the financial spigot a little bit more and so the time has come."

Broader anxieties about the AI industry remain.

Competition from low-cost rival models is growing.

Expensive computer chips, necessary for the industry to operate, are in short supply.

And the US Federal Reserve is concerned about high AI-related debt being financed through "complex and opaque" structures, according to minutes from its October meeting to discuss interest rates, as well as the high concentration of AI exposure among a small number of firms.

Daniela Amodei: Anthropic and the price of AI

Can Anthropic's record breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

#165. Typical energy bill forecast to go up by £443 a year from January as inflation rate rises again

Gas and electricity bills for a typical household are forecast to jump by Â£443 a year from January as the inflation rate rose to its highest level in a year.

A typical annual bill is predicted to rise by 26% in the new year, according to consultancy Cornwall Insight, ahead of the official announcement next week.

Some households will get a Â£100 payment to help with the rising cost of living, a policy which was announced in the Budget.

Prices rose 3.6% in the year to October, up from 3.5% in September mainly because of higher petrol and diesel prices, official figures show.

Inflation has been driven higher in recent months as the US-Israel war with Iran has driven up the cost of energy and fuel.

In response to prices surging, the Bank of England raised interest rates for the first time in three years earlier this month, in a bid to slow inflation to its target of 2%.

Interest rates increased from 3.75% to 4%, and the Bank signalled a further rise could be likely unless the conflict in the Middle East is resolved.

Higher interest rates lift the cost of borrowing for some mortgages, loans and credit cards, but can also lead to a better return on savings.

Emma Reynolds, chief secretary to the Treasury, said the government was providing help for households with energy costs.

VAT has been cut on electricity bills to zero until next July and the government will also hand Â£100 to 20 million homes in January.

But with annual household energy bills forecast to reach Â£2,166, Cornwall Insight's retail markets principal, Craig Lowrey, said that "ultimately this support can only chip away at the edges of a bill that continues to be pushed up by events in the Middle East".

Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh, said: "Affordability is my number one priority, and I will continue driving towards cheap, clean power."

Sophie, who manages a cafe in Brighton, told the BBC she watches her smart meter at home "like a hawk".

"I'm very conscious of the cost of energy, and it is having an impact on how we function in the home as a family," she said.

"I try to cook using the oven a lot less than I used to. We haven't got the heating on yet in our household. We're just like 'wear more layers'. We've got electric blankets on the sofa."

Heating bills, chocolate and cat food: What the inflation figures mean for you

Shadow chancellor, Andrew Griffith, said four successive rises in the inflation rate was down to the UK's "soaring energy costs" and Labour's decision to "hike taxes and employment costs".

Grant Fitzner, chief economist at the ONS, said fuel prices were "once again a major contributor" to the increase in the inflation rate in October.

Rising energy prices can lead to more expensive food and staples for consumers if businesses pass on higher costs.

Earlier this month, the Bank of England said there had been "limited evidence" of this, with some firms absorbing cost increases and accepting lower profit margins.

But Fitzner said on Wednesday: "Annual costs of both raw materials and goods leaving factories continue to climb, driven by higher crude oil and petrol prices, respectively."

Petrol prices rose by 5.2p per litre between September and October, the ONS said, with the average price last month hitting 174.7p per litre - the highest since August 2022.

Diesel prices, meanwhile, reached the highest price since records began 30 years ago, averaging 199.5p per litre in October.

But the ONS said price inflation in restaurants and cafes had slowed last month, and that prices had fallen on average for hotels.

And food price inflation rose by 0.9%, the slowest pace in five years.

Core inflation, which strips out energy and food costs, and services inflation were little changed on last month. The figure is closely watched by the Bank of England when setting interest rates.

It could mean the Bank opts to hold off on a further rate rise in December, though policymakers believe inflation could peak at 4.9% in the middle of next year and not return to 2% until 2028.

In an "adverse scenario", where the US-Iran war could escalate further and send oil prices higher, inflation could reach 6.2% in 2027, it has suggested.

A spokesperson for the Bank said it decided to raise rates two weeks ago to "limit how much higher energy costs affect other prices, and will continue to set interest rates to make sure inflation returns to the 2% target and stays there".

Additional reporting by Raphael Sheridan, economics producer

#164. U.S.-China trade, Taiwan and tech tensions remain unresolved as Trump returns from Asia

SHENZHEN, China — Even as Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation leaders gathered for high-profile photos at the summit's close, key issues from Nvidia to Taiwan remained unaddressed.

"The intrigue is around not what was said, but what remains unresolved," said Gary Dvorchak, managing director at Blueshirt.

"Taiwan remains a major source of strategic uncertainty. North Korea was notably absent from the trilateral nuclear declaration," he said. "Southeast Asia, which is increasingly central to global manufacturing and investment, was overshadowed by major-power diplomacy."

President Donald Trump did not meet with North Korea's leader Kim Jong Un, despite speculation.

Taiwan, one of the 21 member economies of APEC, sent Acer Group founder and honorary chairman Stan Shih to Shenzhen. But the self-governed island, which Beijing claims is part of its territory, did not feature in any significant way during the event. Taiwan is set to hold key elections for Taipei mayor and other posts on Nov. 28.

Instead, the highlight of this week's APEC summit in Shenzhen was a trilateral meeting Thursday between Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin. The three leaders, meeting together for the first time, reaffirmed interests in reducing nuclear risks and plans to increase oil production.

"The optics are impressive, but there's not much happening," Ian Bremmer, president and founder of Eurasia Group, said Friday of the trilateral on CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia."

Resolving the years-long Ukraine war would require involvement from President Volodymyr Zelenskyy and European allies, Bremmer said.

Ahead of the trilateral, Trump and Xi also held their third bilateral summit of the year. Xi greeted Trump at the airport late Wednesday, reciprocating the hospitality shown in September when the Chinese leader visited Washington.

But little was shared publicly on whether the two countries agreed to reduce tariffs further, allow rare earths to keep flowing beyond a January deadline, or address tech competition.

Recent reports tying Jensen Huang to an alleged Chinese spy led to the executive's resignation as Nvidia CEO. It also raised questions in Congress about the effectiveness of U.S. efforts to restrict China's access to advanced chips used to train artificial intelligence.

Trump indicated to Fox News that Nvidia came up briefly in his talks with Xi. U.S. officials last week said Trump did not plan to raise the matter, while noting Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk would accompany the U.S. president.

No other executive was named. Other American business leaders, including Visa CEO Ryan McInerney, who also chairs the U.S.-China Business Council, joined the APEC CEO Summit and was seen at a welcome banquet Wednesday evening.

However, Musk did not appear with Trump at major APEC-related events. CNBC was present for the opening portions of a banquet Wednesday and the Economic Leaders' Meeting Thursday and did not see Musk.

In fact, Musk flew to Shanghai Thursday morning to visit Tesla's factory there, a company executive said on Chinese social media. Musk also showed up in a social media video with Ren Hongbin, chairman of the China Council for the Promotion of International Trade.

Musk addressed Wednesday's DealBook Summit in New York via video conference, having previously said on X that he would be joining from China.

Tesla and the White House did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

"We still don't have a durable resolution of the U.S.-China trade dispute," Dvorchak said, adding: "Business needs certainty, not just ceremony."

Trump visited Shenzhen after briefly traveling to the Philippines on Tuesday for a meeting with the leaders of Australia, India and Japan as part of the Quad grouping happening on the sidelines of the ASEAN Summit. Trump also met the prime minister of Thailand, Anutin Charnvirakul.

Topics of discussion included U.S. cooperation with the region, trade and critical minerals, U.S. Ambassador to ASEAN Kevin Kim told reporters Wednesday. He declined to comment on whether China's influence in the region was addressed.

Kim emphasized it was the second year in a row that Trump visited Southeast Asia, and noted the region "will be at the heart of SI [artificial intelligence] going forward," making it important that American technology is "getting into the right hands" through a regulatory framework the president is building.

Speaking to reporters a day later in Shenzhen, Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi also highlighted the ASEAN and Quad events over APEC. "The major outcome of this diplomatic week was the Quad Summit," she said, noting via official English translation how the four leaders expressed "strong opposition to the unilateral approach to change the status quo by force or coercion ... [as well as] arbitrary export control."

Takaichi, the first Japanese leader in seven years to visit China, received a cool reception upon her arrival in Shenzhen for the APEC meeting.

Tensions between Japan and China have escalated since Takaichi drew Beijing's ire in Nov. 2025 when she said that a Chinese attack on Taiwan could draw a Japanese military response.

Takaichi said Thursday that Japan's position on Taiwan remained unchanged as stated in the 1972 Japan-China Communiqué.

The communique said Japan "understands and respects" the People's Republic of China's position on Taiwan, but does not explicitly acknowledge Taiwan as part of the PRC.

— CNBC's Anniek Bao and Lim Hui Jie contributed to this report.

#163. Why has Waymo pulled all its driverless cars from US freeways?

Waymo has temporarily suspended driverless rides on US freeways after California banned the service over safety concerns.

California's Department of Motor Vehicles said a series of traffic incidents, including robotaxis entering active construction zones, posed an imminent risk to public safety. Waymo has now restricted freeway operations in all other US markets while it reviews the performance of its vehicles.

Waymo currently operates in 15 US cities, though only offered driverless freeway rides in a handful of cities, including San Francisco and Los Angeles.

#162. Target shares rise 7% as retailer posts best sales growth in five years, hikes guidance

Target on Wednesday reported its best sales growth in more than five years and beat Wall Street's revenue expectations, the latest signs of its turnaround strategy taking hold.

The retailer said its net sales grew 7.1% during the fiscal third quarter. Comparable sales rose 5.6%, compared with StreetAccount estimates of up 3.8%. That growth was boosted by a 5% increase in comparable traffic, Target added.

"[It's] a healthy sign that guests are responding to our changes and choosing Target more often," CEO Michael Fiddelke told reporters.

Digital comparable sales jumped nearly 10% during the quarter.

Encouraged by the growth, Target raised its full-year guidance, upping its net sales growth guidance by 1 percentage point to around 6%. It's also expecting full-year operating income margin rate to be around 6.2%, which includes roughly 1.1 percentage points of benefit from tariff refunds.

The company also said it's now projecting full-year earnings per share to be in a range of $10.65 to $11.40, compared with prior guidance of between $9.90 and $10.90.

"The changes we've made across our assortment and guest experience are adding up," Fiddelke told reporters. "While we're encouraged by the progress, we know there's more work ahead to sustain this momentum and deliver consistent growth over time."

Target shares climbed about 7% in afternoon trading Wednesday.

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter ended Oct. 31, Target reported net income of $1.21 billion, or $2.65 per share, up from $689 million, or $1.51 per share, a year earlier.

This year, the company said it's invested approximately $3.8 billion in capital expenditures, up nearly $1 billion from last year. Because of those returns, Fiddelke said Target is now expecting to invest between $5 billion and $6 billion in capex next year.

On a call with analysts, he added that those investments will go toward more store remodels, technology, supply chain improvements, among other initiatives.

The company said it saw particular strength in beauty, which saw 9% growth, and food and beverage, where sales increased nearly 7%. The retailer added that net sales rose in all three months of the third quarter, with the strongest performance during the back-to-school season.

Fiddelke told reporters the quarter's strength showed "continued progress" toward Target's priorities that it outlined at the beginning of its turnaround.

As it looks to the holiday season, Target said it's marketing thousands of products priced at $5 and under, along with new artificial intelligence-powered shopping experiences to drive engagement. Fiddelke said Target is "encouraged" by shopping behaviors it's seen so far in the current quarter.

The big-box retailer has been showing signs of returning to growth, as it sees stronger sales trends after years of flat revenue. The results come after Target last quarter hiked its full fiscal-year outlook and said it saw "broad-based" strength across its various categories.

It's also lowered prices on more than 10,000 items in an effort to get customers back into its stores, especially as consumer sentiment lags and inflation remains high. The retailer said on Wednesday that it cut prices on an additional roughly 2,000 items during the third quarter.

Target's stock is up more than 70% this year.

#160. Gilts v kilts: What is happening with Scottish government bonds?

The Scottish government is facing questions about its plan to issue its first ever bonds.

The move would allow ministers to borrow money from investors who in return receive regular interest payments.

The aim is to raise about Â£1.5bn by the end of the parliamentary term, with the funds to be used on long-term projects such as building hospitals or maintaining roads.

Named "kilts", a nod to UK government "gilts", the scheme has provoked much debate.

This week, MSPs on Holyrood's finance committee heard about the potential risks and rewards.

A bond is a financial tool used by a government to borrow money. They are sold with a promise for the buyer, or investor, to be paid back at the end of an agreed term.

The government will also make regular payments to the investor.

In the UK, a government bond is called a gilt. They are typically bought by financial institutions such as pension funds, insurance companies and commercial banks.

As a play on this term, the Scottish bonds have been nicknamed "kilts".

The Scottish government has said it expects the bonds to be issued within this financial year, subject to market conditions.

Former First Minister Humza Yousaf commissioned initial work in 2023 after advisers in the Scottish government's investor panel recommended making bonds available to the market as a means of raising Scotland's profile and attracting investment.

In November last year, the government announced that it had received an identical credit rating to the UK's score.

This was based on "prudent fiscal management" and the country's economic stability - though ministers were warned this could change if Scotland moved towards independence.

With market volatility due to conflicts in Ukraine and the Middle East, some have questioned whether now is a good time to launch bonds.

But at Holyrood, David McMillan, professor in finance and accounting at the University of Stirling, told MSPs: "You can't wait for a perfect time because there's going to be no perfect time."

The government wants to raise Â£1.5bn through bonds by 2031, although that would only be a fraction of its near Â£70bn budget.

About four-fifths of the Scottish government's budget is used on resource spending, which goes towards day-to-day costs such as funding the NHS, benefits payments and paying salaries.

A smaller portion is made up of capital spending, typically used on long-term physical assets such as buildings and bridges, or maintaining roads or paths.

The Scottish government is unable to borrow for resource spending other than to correct forecasting errors.

However, for capital spending, it can borrow as much as Â£450m a year at 2023-24 levels up to a total cap of Â£3bn - to be uprated with inflation.

Adjusted for inflation, the government could borrow about Â£491m in the current financial year, with a total limit of about Â£3.3bn.

Economists have noted that Scottish bonds would be a more costly way of borrowing than existing routes provided by the UK government through the National Loans Fund.

It has been estimated, external that interest rates on "kilts" would be 0.3 percentage points above gilts, plus an additional Â£2m per bond in administrative costs.

The Scottish government has not released costings, citing market sensitivities, but has insisted, external issuing bonds would represent value for money relative to the costs of borrowing through the UK scheme.

Ministers argue that issuing bonds would be a good opportunity to market the country to investors, and would be helpful if Scotland became independent.

Dr Ben Cormier, a senior lecturer at the University of Strathclyde's department of government and public policy, told Holyrood's finance committee that while the Scottish bonds could cost marginally more than the UK scheme, they would "bring flexibility to public finances and debt management".

McMillan agreed with the plans "in principle", but warned they would lose "credibility" with investors if limited to five years.

Cormier warned that independence was the one thing "that could materially change the nature of risk associated with Scottish bonds".

Conservative MSP Liam Kerr said bonds could be more expensive than borrowing through the National Loans Fund and voiced concerns about a lack of transparency in the programme.

McMillan agreed there should be "full information" available, saying: "You've got to have a clear, full and frank conversation with the public."

Scottish government aiming to issue first bonds in 2026-27

MSPs 'unable to say' if Scottish government spending plans are affordable

#159. Elon Musk says record $446m spending on US midterms was 'pretty foolish'

Elon Musk said on Wednesday that he felt "pretty foolish" about the record sum he spent on the US midterm elections, following sweeping Republican defeats.

The Tesla and SpaceX chief executive was the election's top individual donor, giving $446m (Â£339m) to support President Donald Trump's party, particularly in Texas where many of Musk's firms are based.

Musk, who had backed Trump's second White House bid, also said he was "not Maga" - the Make America Great Again movement associated with the president.

Democrats won control of both chambers in the US Congress, flipping both swing seats and making historic gains in places that have for decades been controlled by Republicans.

The election was seen as a bruising defeat for Trump, who has faced pressure over the impact his policies have had on the cost of living in the US.

Musk was speaking virtually, external at the New York Times DealBook Summit, an annual conference hosted in the city attended by major business and political leaders.

"I'm not Maga," Musk said, referring to the right-wing political movement.

He added: "I'm not sure what Maga means... if that means sort of blind adherence to a set of policies by the Republican Party, then I certainly don't have that."

This marks a major shift in tone by Musk, who has previously been seen wearing a Maga hat.

"I'm not just Maga, I'm dark, gothic Maga," he joked at a Trump rally in 2024.

Musk has worn a Maga hat in public on several occasions

For several months at the start of Trump's second term in the White House, Musk served as an unpaid "special government employee" and Senior Advisor to the President, leading the Department of Government Efficiency (Doge) to cut government spending.

In October 2026, the Pentagon named Musk as a co-lead of a future of warfare study called Project Meridian, while he continued to travel with and advise Trump.

Musk went on to say he did not see much for the money he had spent on the Republicans' campaign, though he added that it was likely that candidates would have fared worse without the spending.

The large number of Republican losses could have implications for Musk and his businesses.

Democrats coming into power in both the Senate and House of Representatives have pledged greater scrutiny of tech companies, including over the building of controversial data centres.

In Georgia, where SpaceX has data centre operations, the newly elected Democrat governor promised to limit the rollout of the massive buildings, as well as the tax breaks tech companies often get to build them.

Democratic lawmakers have also suggested that SpaceX, which has gained an estimated $10bn in new government contracts since Trump was re-elected, should be investigated.

Watch: Key moments from the US midterm election results

One of the most striking midterms wins went to Democrat James Talarico, who beat Republican Ken Paxton for a US Senate seat in Texas.

According to BBC analysis, Musk had spent at least $45m in support of Paxton.

Texas is where key operations for Musk's car company Tesla and rocket and technology firm SpaceX are based.

Musk said on Wednesday that he believes Texas' new governor, Democrat Gina Hinojosa, is "going to be quite moderate".

Hinojosa defeated Republican Governor Greg Abbott, ending more than three decades of the party's control of the state.

The Tesla boss added : "I don't think she's a far-left governor. And based on everything I've heard about her, I think things will be fine in Texas with Gina as governor."

#158. Bitcoin suffered identical sell-off pattern ahead of Anthropic, SpaceX IPOs — what happens next?

Bitcoin has suffered nearly identical sell-offs ahead of two of the biggest public offerings this year, leaving investors wondering if competing demand for investor capital could be weighing on the cryptocurrency and perhaps delaying its recovery.

The price of the bitcoin dropped 22.7% in the 30 days before Anthropic's IPO and fell 22.3% in the month leading up to SpaceX's June 12 listing. Ether declined 25.3% in the 30 days preceding Anthropic's debut, and 28% in the same window ahead of SpaceX.

"Liquidity events can elevate correlations across asset classes quickly," Matthew Sheffield, chief investment officer at the ether treasury firm Sharplink, told CNBC. "The first 15 min of [Thursday's] Anthropic IPO put that on full display. Tech stocks, value stocks, oil, gold, crypto all sold off as traders sold assets to buy shares in the secondary market."

"Stores of value function as such over long horizons, but in the short term are still liquidity driven," Sheffield said. "We saw this in early Covid when the market fled to cash. Stock, bonds, commodities ... it didn't matter, you were being sold for cash."

While investors appear to be rotating capital out of crypto to make room for history-making IPO investments, that can't be said with precise certainty. Crypto has also been under pressure from ETF outflows, leverage, macro uncertainty and broader risk-off sentiment.

Still, the post-IPO price action may offer a data point in understanding where bitcoin's price goes next. With Anthropic now public, investors are watching to see whether bitcoin and ether find a floor, and if institutional demand returns.

"[After] the SpaceX IPO, bitcoin didn't truly find its final bottom until after the IPO was completed, and it didn't actually reverse the downtrend until 70 days later," said Matt Cole, CEO and chairman of Strive Asset Management, a bitcoin treasury firm. "My view is that [for] bitcoin, the most likely scenario is that it chops around here."

The expiration of IPO allocation restrictions can influence the timing of capital flows into digital assets, Sheffield said.

"Post SpaceX we saw the wealth effect in full force," Sheffield said. "Many brokers make traders wait 15-30 days before they are allowed to flip shares they received as allocations. … If you went long ETH versus SpaceX on the day of the IPO, on day 31 was when you would have become profitable."

Bitcoin's next critical test is between $60,000 and $62,000, according to Nicolai Sondergaard, a research analyst at Nansen. On Friday, it was sitting at about $62,000.

As of Friday, bitcoin was hovering at the upper end of a critical $60,000 to $62,000 support range. Its next move could determine whether it can stabilize or if it will face another leg lower and revisit its July low of $57,737.35.

Throughout November, bitcoin has been struggling from a combination of dollar strength, institutional selling, and ongoing leveraged bitcoin liquidations. The declines began in late October, as concerns over artificial intelligence valuations and spending fueled a broad tech sell-off. They were compounded by the $6.3 billion Binance hack and weakening institutional ETF demand.

Bitcoin is in the early stages of forming a bottom, according to Cole. Retail fear is elevated amid recent outages, quantum concerns and other short-term uncertainties, but bitcoin should continue to benefit from macro forces like rising debt and weakening fiat currencies, which should drive price higher, he said.

"Capital markets are open, … retail fears are high, and when you combine those things it's much more likely to be a dip-buying opportunity across the board, including bitcoin, than the start of a great financial crisis," said Cole. "It'll likely age as a volatile time to consider allocating to risk, not the opposite."

"The winter for bitcoin is basically at the end, but after winter does not come summer, it comes early spring," he added. "Anytime anyone's been around early spring, what you notice is it gets really cold still, it still rains – it's not necessarily great weather, and I think that's the environment we're in for bitcoin."

#156. Viagogo may have broken law on hidden fees, watchdog provisionally finds

Ticket-reselling site Viagogo may have broken the law on hidden fees, the UK's competition body has said in a provisional finding.

The Competition and Markets Authority (CMA) said it found that Viagogo failed to show consumers the total price upfront when they were buying tickets "and added fees later in the process, a practice known as drip pricing".

The CMA added that this was an initial finding, and it should not be assumed that the company had actually broken the law.

In a statement, Viagogo said it was co-operating fully with the CMA, adding that the investigation was continuing and "no final findings have been made".

The CMA said last year that it was investigating a number of companies over their online pricing practices, including Viagogo.

The competition watchdog said it had looked into the experience of UK fans buying tickets for events such as concerts and sport on three websites that operate under the Viagogo brand.

"The CMA believes that mandatory costs such as taxes and booking fees were added at later stages of the checkout process and not included in the total price from the start," it said.

"Drip pricing can cause fans to be caught out by extra charges and buy tickets they might not otherwise have selected, had they been able to compare prices accurately from the start."

The CMA said Viagogo would now have an opportunity to consider its findings and respond before a final decision is made.

"This is an ongoing investigation and no final findings have been made," Viagogo said in a statement.

"We have engaged constructively with the CMA and continue to cooperate fully with its investigation."

The CMA has powers to tackle anti-competitive behaviour. It can now decide if consumer laws have been broken - rather than having to go through the courts.

Drip pricing is one of the practices the CMA has been focusing on particularly.

On Wednesday, it said it was investigating three companies, external over the issue, and in August it announced that the Trainline, Virgin Atlantic and RED Driving School had been engaged in the practice.

In general, the CMA can impose penalties of up to 10% of global turnover for a breach of consumer law. However, it will consider a number of factors, including the severity of conduct, before deciding on the size of the penalty.

In June this year, the watchdog ordered rival ticket reseller StubHub UK to refund more than 50,000 customers and pay a Â£900,000 fine for not showing people the total price upfront when buying tickets.

StubHub UK received a 40% reduction to its financial penalty after it admitted to breaking the law and agreed to settle the case early with the CMA.

#153. The seven Anthropic founders who are about to become multi-billionaires

Brother and sister Dario and Daniela Amodei are chief executive and president of Anthropic

In the space of just five years, Anthropic has gone from unknown start-up to tech giant, worth more than a trillion dollars.

The company behind the AI platform Claude was formed by seven people who left rival OpenAI in 2021 and, in a matter of hours, will become multi-billionaires - on paper at least.

Anthropic is floating the business on the US stock exchange and will sell its shares at $83 each, it said on Wednesday.

At that price, the founders' shareholdings will be worth at least $19.3bn (Â£14.7bn) each.

So, who are these people, all in their thirties and forties, who are about to become incredibly wealthy?

In 2020, Dario Amodei was working for OpenAI, the firm that brought generative artificial intelligence to a mainstream audience, when it launched Chat-GPT.

He decided he wanted to do things his own way, and left, taking his sister Daniela, and five colleagues with him.

Amodei grew up in San Francisco, impressing school friends not just with his academic prowess but also his speed on the running track, and his preoccupation with philosophical and technological questions including the potential of machines to gain consciousness and human longevity.

Initially he studied physics at Princeton and Stanford. But following his father's death from hepatitis C, he switched to biology and biomedical sciences.

Amodei did stints at the Chinese tech giant Baidu and Google before joining OpenAI.

But he began to worry that some people within the company were putting profit before safety, prompting the decision to jump ship.

Now 43, he is Anthropic's chief executive, and the person who, in his sister's words, makes the company "ambitious".

Dario and Daniela Amodei, were close growing up and later shared a flat together in San Francisco with other friends. She followed him into OpenAI and joined him when he left to found Anthropic.

She and her brother have always genuinely liked each other, she says, but the only thing she ever did first, was when he followed her in becoming vegetarian.

Amodei studied English Literature at the University of California Santa Cruz, going on to work for Democrat representative Matt Cartwright.

She has also worked at financial services platform Stripe, where she was head of risk operations.

The 39-year-old is president of Anthropic, manages the executive team, and leads the firm's research, engineering, product, governance and commercial work.

Tom Brown, now 39, followed the Amodei siblings when they left to found their new venture.

He led GPT-3 engineering at OpenAI, and prior to that was at Google on its DeepMind project.

Brown established his entrepreneurial credentials co-founding an app called "Grouper" that helps set up drinks between groups of friends.

He has supported US President Donald Trump's stance on datacentres, arguing that they are "an enormous source of prosperity".

Now as chief compute officer at Anthropic he is responsible for how the company secures and uses its computing resources.

Jack Clark, is another arts graduate among the original start-up's founding members and the only one who is not from North America.

Originally from Brighton in the UK, he studied English Literature and Creative Writing at the University of East Anglia and became a technology journalist, writing for publications including ZDNet UK, The Register, and Bloomberg.

He was caught up in the excitement of artificial intelligence and joined OpenAI, where he worked initially in communications, later becoming its policy director.

Now, 37, Clark is head of public benefit at Anthropic. He also leads the Anthropic Institute, an AI think tank.

Jared Kaplan, 42, is chief science officer at Anthropic, and heads its technical research. He is also an associate professor at Johns Hopkins University in Baltimore.

He holds a PhD in physics from Harvard University.

His work on scaling laws - which describe mathematical patterns that show that bigger AI models trained on more data tend to be more consistent - has helped provide AI firms with a framework for predicting AI behaviour, according to Anthropic.

Chris Olah is described as "interpretability research lead" primarily focused on how AI models work with each other.

But according to the New York Times, Olah has spent much of the last year convening meetings with faith leaders from across the religious spectrum.

Described by the newspaper as "boyish" but with "the zeal of an evangelist" the 34-year-old is reportedly exploring the relationship between morality and AI.

He previously led interoperability research at OpenAI, and prior to that was a research scientist at Google Brain.

The Canadian left the University of Toronto early, but later secured a Thiel Fellowship of $100,000, which is a scholarship to help gifted people move into research without a degree.

Sam McCandlish, 36, takes less of a public-facing role than the other founders, but like them will own around 1.6% of the company after its shares go on sale. As a result he too will see his stake crystallise into a hugely valuable asset.

McCandlish is Anthropic's chief architect, and helps shape and implement the company's policy designed to mitigate the risks of AI. Prior to this he worked on safety at OpenAI.

He has a physics PhD from Stanford, and maths and physics degree from Brandeis University outside Boston.

Power players: How do you value Anthropic?

#152. Hims & Hers launches new peptide treatments — while admitting there's more to learn about efficacy

Hims & Hers Health on Wednesday said it will offer five formulations of peptides and longevity treatments compounded in the U.S. as part of the initial rollout of its Peptides+ platform, all of which are not approved by regulators.

Even as it announced the new products, Hims & Hers acknowledged it has more to learn about how best to use the peptides and how effective they are — underscoring the uncertainty surrounding the growing space.

The new offerings come after the federal government eased restrictions on compounding certain unapproved peptides, which are short chains of amino ​acids. Those products have exploded in popularity among fitness and longevity enthusiasts who attest that they help with muscle growth, rapid injury recovery and anti-aging, even though most lack evidence from robust human trials.

Hims in late October launched an early access list for the upcoming peptide offerings, and more than 10,000 people signed up within 15 days, a company spokesperson said in a statement.

None of the company's specific compounded products are approved by the Food and Drug Administration. But Hims said they are authorized by law and formulated by pharmacies "that adhere to rigorous quality standards and are licensed and inspected by state boards of pharmacy."

The products include compounded formulations of sermorelin, a peptide that stimulates growth-hormone release and is marketed for recovery, body composition and anti-aging; glutathione, a peptide promoted ‌for anti-aging and athletic recovery; and nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide, or NAD+, a coenzyme that plays a role in cellular metabolism.

The company will also offer compounded versions of vitamin B-12, a vitamin needed to make red blood cells, build DNA, and maintain healthy nerve function, and L-carnitine, an amino acid derivative that helps the body turn fat into energy. The FDA has approved certain prescription forms of both peptides for established medical uses, primarily for diagnosed deficiencies in those compounds.

Hims said it plans to add more peptides and therapies made in the U.S. over time. That includes BPC-157, an unapproved peptide derived from a protein found in human gastric juice that is marketed for healing processes. The company also aims to introduce another unapproved peptide called TB-500, which is derived from a naturally occurring protein in the body and is marketed for broader, systemic soft-tissue recovery and flexibility. Most of the research on both has been conducted in animals, not humans.

To receive the products, members on the Hims platform will start with an assessment of health history and goals with a licensed provider trained in peptide therapies, who can tailor a personalized plan to their needs, if appropriate.

The company said it intends to use its communication platform to check in with patients and ensure they are seeing desired results.

"If they're not, we need to do something about it," Dr. Anant Vinjamoori, Hims chief medical officer, told CNBC. "We need to, you know, adjust the dose when that's appropriate, or in some cases consider other compounds that's maybe a better fit. And of course, there will also be cases when we need to refer people to other care options."

However, Vinjamoori said there's still uncertainty over how to best use the peptides.

"We don't have all of this data that we're used to having with compounds like this," he said. "We have the opportunity to get it right, and I think we see that as our responsibility with a company of our size, our skill, and resources to really lead."

Vinjamoori added that people who have serious illnesses, such as cardiovascular disease, liver disease and kidney disease, will not be candidates for the compounds for now.

Meanwhile, Andrew Huberman, a well known neuroscientist, podcaster and Stanford University School of Medicine professor who recently joined Hims' board of directors, in a statement called compounds "real medical treatments, not just a wellness trend." He said Hims provides safe access through "trusted sourcing and physician oversight."

But health experts raised concerns about the broader mainstream marketing of peptides in the U.S. and accessing them on telehealth platforms.

"With peptides, I think you have to view them the same way you would view any drug that hasn't been rigorously tested," said Joshua Kritzer, a chemistry professor at Tufts University. "Without actual testing, all you have is anecdotal information, and that's just not enough information to put a substance in your body."

Kritzer said the lack of large clinical trials means researchers can't rule out potentially serious, rare complications from peptides — the kinds of risks that have led regulators to reject or pull otherwise effective drugs from the market after they underwent comprehensive testing in humans.

Without that testing, it's also unclear how telehealth firms, physicians and pharmacies can make decisions about what doses patients should take, said Paul Knoepfler, a cell biology and human anatomy professor at UC Davis Health. While some people rely on anecdotal data from real peptide users, "the dosing for these products really has not been established," he added.

Knoepfler said one risk is that injecting too much of a synthetic peptide into patients could trigger "weird immune responses that are really bad for one's health" because the body might recognize it as foreign.

"With the peptides, it's very loosey-goosey," Knoepfler told CNBC. "How does one even know what would be the effective amount for whatever you're hoping it will do, and what amount would have more risks?"

But he said the more contact patients can have with physicians around taking a compounded peptide, the better. Providers, including those tied to telehealth firms, should make sure patients are well-informed about the possible risks and unknowns of a given product and consider their preexisting conditions, cancer history and other medications, Knoepfler said.

That appears to be consistent with the approach Hims & Hers is taking. The company says patients who use the peptide and longevity products can also use its Care+ offering, which pairs treatment with ongoing support. That includes twice-a-year lab testing to track their progress and the ability to message providers trained in peptide therapy around the clock with any questions.

Still, Kritzer argued that having a doctor who only knows a patient from online, essentially mail-order platforms or websites "is not enough supervision to put you on a prescription medication," especially an unapproved one.

Hims' ability to legally sell some of these peptides stems from a major shift in federal oversight by the Health and Human Services Department. It issued a policy in October that says the Food and Drug Administration will not take enforcement action against licensed pharmacies, federal facilities or physicians that compound and dispense six unapproved peptides while formal rules are reviewed.

In practice, that means these pharmacies and other firms can now offer them, even though the peptides aren't yet on the FDA's official list of ingredients compounding pharmacies are allowed to make. It does not translate to formal approval of the specific peptides.

The policy followed a 2023 decision by the Biden administration where the FDA restricted compounding pharmacies from producing popular peptide formulations due to safety, impurity and immune-reaction concerns.

HHS Secretary ​Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has supported broader access to peptides, calling himself a "big fan" of the treatments and arguing that restrictions were driving Americans to potentially unsafe products from overseas. Kennedy has previously described using BPC-157 to treat a muscle injury.

The announcement also comes after Hims in February 2025 acquired a California-based peptide manufacturing facility equipped with advanced peptide synthesizers.

The evidence for peptide efficacy varies by compound.

A 2026 systematic review screening 113 studies, including both small trials on humans along with rodent research, found zero eligible clinical outcome trials proving that administering IV or intramuscular NAD+ improves anti-aging, energy, or wellness endpoints in humans.

There is some evidence for specific uses of certain peptides, such as potential weight loss. A review of 37 L-carnitine trials found participants lost about 2.7 pounds on average, though that analysis does not demonstrate that a compounded injection works the same way.

Meanwhile, vitamin B-12 injections are most commonly used to help people with a diagnosed deficiency rather than preventative care. U.S. survey data shows only 3.6% of adults are B-12 deficient.

Glutathione's evidence is thin, too. IV injections of it are widely marketed for skin whitening, yet not FDA-approved for this purpose.

A small study published in the Journal of Pakistan Association of Dermatologists evaluating IV glutathione showed that the treatment is "not very effective" for skin-lightening, and that it loses efficacy with time.

The FDA also said in August that it received reports of at least 30 patients who experienced adverse events after they received compounded IV glutathione alone or in combination with other ingredients from different compounding pharmacies.

All of the glutathione tied to the reports were from the same wholesale distributor and was labeled as dietary supplement grade, which is not an appropriate ingredient to make injectable drugs. Two of the Texas pharmacies conducted recalls related to elevated endotoxin levels in their glutathione injectable drugs.

These studies and reports don't speak to how Hims' exact combination will perform, but they do highlight the current limits on data studying the treatments and the questions surrounding them.

#151. Data center construction costs soar 13% in 2026

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

The demand for large-scale data centers in the U.S. this year, driven by the need for computing power for artificial intelligence, grew well beyond the supply of powered land and the labor to build these structures. It also pushed timelines and risk for contractors.

All that resulted in costs rising 13% year-over-year to more than twice the global rate, according to a new report from Turner & Townsend, a global project management company. The gain this year was about three times the last ten-year average.

The report looked at over 500 live or recently completed projects in 62 global markets. It also included a survey of 400 experts in the global data center industry.

While there was some stabilization in cost inflation this year, it was not enough to offset the huge growth in not just demand but speed of construction and delivery.

"More aggressive schedules tie to contractors in particular and subcontractors baking in more risk allowances in their pricing," said Paul Barry, North America data center lead for Turner & Townsend. "So whether that's acceleration allowances, whether that's more overtime and labor attraction allowances, even just higher fees for taking on the risk of delivering at a faster pace than what they would traditionally take on."

All of those things, combined with the spike in demand and the strain on the supply chain, translate into higher costs, he added.

Capital expenditure on global data centers is projected to rise from about $800 billion this year to $1.8 trillion per year in 2050, according to a recent report from PwC. The U.S. is expected to capture almost half of all global investment in AI infrastructure, which includes both data centers and chips.

Power is, of course, key to all data centers, and 56% of those surveyed by Turner & Townsend globally said it was a major obstacle to delivery. Just over half said utility connections typically take between one and two years, and almost a third reported timeframes of over two years. Costs for power vary regionally — from $15.01 per watt in Silicon Valley to $10.51 per watt in Dallas, based on a typical 60-megawatt design.As with every type of construction in the U.S., labor continues to be a significant headwind. Just over one quarter of respondents said their contractor capacity, that is, the ability to manage construction backlogs, was sufficient.

Going forward, Barry said he expects costs to moderate somewhat, with cost increases in the 6% to 10% range in the next year. A lot of the major projects have already started or are starting now, so cost risk has been baked into those contracts.

"If this demand continues, then there'll be more continued flow of project starts and so on, but I think we've hit a big surge in 2026, especially of new big major projects that have been procured," said Barry.

#149. Crew unaware of risks before Mike Lynch's yacht Bayesian sank, say investigators

There were 22 people on board the Bayesian at the time of the incident

The crew of the Bayesian superyacht did not know about critical safety risks that left it vulnerable in powerful winds before it sank off the coast in northern Sicily, killing seven people, British investigators say.

British tech tycoon Mike Lynch, 59, and his daughter Hannah, 18, were among seven people who died when the vessel sank on 19 August 2024.

The sinking of the Bayesian unfolded with "extraordinary speed" when a violent downburst of freak hurricane-force winds knocked it onto its side in less than 15 seconds, the UK's Marine Accident Investigation Branch (MAIB) final report said.

Safety information provided to the crew did not identify this type of risk, the MAIB said.

"It was very dynamic, very quick," said MAIB principal inspector Simon Graves. "The speed of events overtook the crew and prevented them from taking any other action."

The accident also led to the deaths of Morgan Stanley International bank chairman Jonathan Bloomer, 70, and his wife, Judy, 71; American lawyer Chris Morvillo, 59, and his wife Neda Morvillo, 57; and crew member Rick Thomas, 59.

Mike Lynch and his daughter Hannah both died when the Bayesian sank

Rob Loder, chief inspector of marine accidents said: "Our thoughts remain with the families and loved ones of the seven who lost their lives, and with all those affected by the loss of Bayesian. This accident developed with extraordinary speed."

Fifteen survivors escaped from the 56-metre (184ft) luxury yacht and managed to get to a life raft that had been launched by a crew member.

The marine investigation found that the Bayesian "had a vulnerability to high winds that was not recognised, because the yacht had never been assessed at anchor with its sails down".

In addition, "the extreme winds that night were also not explicitly forecast, so the crew had no warning of the risk", Loder added.

WATCH: Millionaire Superyacht: Why Ships Sink

In the early hours of 19 August 2024 the Bayesian was anchored outside of Porticello port, on the north coast of Sicily.

With thunderstorms rapidly coming in, wind speeds went from 8 knots to about 70 knots (80mph) within minutes. The weather forecast had only predicted gusts of up to 27 knots (31mph).

The MAIB report stated that with no sails set Bayesian was vulnerable to being knocked over in severe winds.

It also found that any knockdown - where the boat was suddenly tipped sideways - would be "irrecoverable once the yacht passed the angle of vanishing stability", assessed in the study as being 73 degrees. Once the vessel passed this angle it could not right itself.

The wreckage of the Bayesian off the coast of Porticello, Sicily

In addition, the centreboard, which provides stability when a ship is under sail, was raised. The report said the centreboard was not needed when the Bayesian was using its motors and was rarely lowered at anchor. The way the boat was loaded, and the angle of the wind, also contributed to the accident.

Inspectors said a "downburst of hurricane-force winds" was the most likely cause of the ship keeling over.

They happen when cold, dense air plunges rapidly, sending out violent winds across the surface.

The MAIB said that none of risks were covered in the UK training syllabus, nor in published guidance available to crew.

Once the vessel was on its side, it rapidly filled with water and sank.

As it heeled over, five people were injured, either by falling or by items falling on them and a deckhand was thrown into the sea, the report said.

As water started entering the yacht, two of the guests "used the furniture drawers as an improvised ladder to exit into the central alleyway", then climbed along the internal walls to get into the saloon.

The MAIB's final report into the sinking, published on Thursday, appeared to contrast with recent reports from Italy, where prosecution experts have reportedly blamed errors by the crew rather than problems with the yacht.

The investigation was carried out alongside a criminal probe in Italy, where captain James Cutfield, chief engineer Tim Parker Eaton and deckhand Matthew Griffith are under investigation over potential charges of multiple manslaughter and culpable shipwreck.

British investigators said that Italian prosecutors had restricted access to some witnesses and evidence.

"It is possible that the circumstances depicted in this report may alter following disclosure of evidence withheld by the Italian authorities," the report says.

However, the MAIB's Graves said he was satisfied with the British investigation.

"We are content with our investigation, the investigation that we've undertaken, and the information we've used to feed into it."

The report also found that alcohol consumption had not contributed to the sinking.

MAIB investigator Simon Graves: "This was an accident and there were a number of contributory factors"

The MAIB report makes seven recommendations to four agencies. It calls for new regulations to be set for single-hulled sailing vessels, and new rules around life rafts.

It also urges collaboration between the Met Office and its global counterpart to improve information-sharing of severe weather forecasts with mariners.

The Maritime and Coastguard Agency is also investigating the sinking.

#146. Prediction markets outlook for stocks in 2026 dims amid AI sell-off

As stocks continue their tech-driven sell-off, prediction markets traders are seeing a bleaker outlook for the remainder of the year.

On platform Kalshi, speculators place just 6% odds that the S&P 500 will cross 8,000 in 2026. On Oct. 13, those odds were as high as 72%.

Contracts ask traders if the S&P 500 will go above various levels in 2026, and they're resolved using data from S&P Dow Jones Indices.

On Friday, the Nasdaq Composite turned negative for the year as investors continued to dump tech stocks, primarily those with exposure to the artificial intelligence build-out. The Dow Jones Industrial Average and the S&P 500 fell into correction territory, too.

The S&P 500 has slashed its year-to-date gain to just over 2%.

The Stock Trader's Almanac in a Thursday note broadly agreed that the hopes for a December rally are fading, with the caveat that a "Santa rally" could still appear at the end of the year.

"The market is under pressure and more downside can't be ruled out," the note said.

The Stock Trader's Almanac sees 2026 following the year-end pattern of 2018, when the Federal Reserve was in a tightening cycle in a midterm year. The Fed is widely expected to hike rates at its December meeting.

In that scenario, the Stock Trader's Almanac thinks more churn is to come. "The low is more likely in mid- to late December than this week."

#145. McDonald's sex abuse: 'They were waiting for the younger girls to turn 18'

Ex-McDonald's worker: "You need to fix it so it doesn't happen again"

A former McDonald's worker who says a manager failed to protect her when she was being sexualised by customers, has called on the firm to âchange the way they treat younger employeesâ.

Hope, who worked at a branch in the West Midlands in 2023, also told BBC News she saw older colleagues eyeing up 16- and 17-year-old workers, and talking about wanting to sleep with them when they turned 18.

Hope is one of hundreds of workers bringing a discrimination claim against McDonald's. This week, a judge ruled against McDonald's attempt to have their claims struck out, a move that Hope welcomed.

McDonald's said: "Ensuring a safe, respectful and inclusive working environment across our restaurants and offices remains a fundamental priority."

The latest developments come after a BBC investigation in 2023 which heard that workers, some as young as 17, were being groped and harassed.

Last year, McDonald's staff told us they still faced sexual abuse and harassment.

McDonald's workers speak out over sexual abuse claims

McDonald's boss on abuse claims: 'I don't want to talk about the past'

Every McDonald's warned over staff sexual abuse

Hope, who is now 22, says that her branch of McDonalds had a very sexualised, "toxic" environment.

She spoke of a time a manager came up behind her and put his hands on her shoulders. From then on, whenever she saw him coming over, she says she "moved quick" to get away from him.

Another time, she says a manager failed to protect her when she says she was being sexualised and objectified by two customers on a night shift.

Hope says she told the manager what was happening, but he told her to "just suck it up and deal with it".

Her main concern, however, was for the younger teenagers, who she says weren't being looked after.

"You could tell there were other staff members waiting for their 18th birthdays. That sort of like toxic culture where it's like, 'Oh, you know, it's not illegal now.' It was that feeling from quite a few of them," she says.

"And why would you? That is literally a child still."

Hundreds of current and former McDonald's employees are bringing a discrimination claim against the firm.

The fast-food chain had attempted to have the complaints thrown out but this week at the London Central Employment Tribunal, Judge Glennie ruled that 450 cases can go ahead and they are now expected to be heard at the Employment Tribunal in April.

The BBC understands that these relate to indirect discrimination, not sexual harassment.

Hope told us she was not taking part in the legal case for money, saying they didn't know if they were going to get anything.

Instead, she said it was to try and "change the way they treat younger employees".

"It's going to change the way they treat allegations. It's going to shake up the whole internal process and make life easier for the kids who are now having their first jobs. And it's going to make itâ¯â¯a lot safer, I think, as well.â¯â¯And that's something you can't put a price on, the safety of the young these days.''

She also accused McDonald's of trying to "delay the inevitable" by attempting to have the claims thrown out.

"And the judge quite rightly looked at them and said: 'No. We're not going to do that.'"

The claims were presented by law firm Leigh Day, which is acting for the current and former employees.

The claims focus on the vulnerability of young or female workers in the workplace and follow allegations of widespread harassment and discrimination across McDonald's restaurants in the UK.

Leigh Day partner Kiran Daurka told the BBC: "We're really delighted that the judge has found that these claims can go forward to a full hearing. It's really important for access to justice that these cases are heard."

A McDonald's spokesperson said they could not comment on the specifics of ongoing legal proceedings.

In a statement, they said the company had "embedded an extensive set of robust and far-reaching initiatives" as part of its "steadfast commitment" to its staff, adding that this work continues.

"Any form of harassment, discrimination or inappropriate behaviour is unacceptable and has no place in our restaurants or offices. We are confident that with the guidance and partnership of external experts, we and our franchisees have industry leading safeguarding measures in place," the spokesperson added.
