#142. Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

It was during November 2020 that somewhere in San Francisco, a woman called Daniela Amodei phoned a friend, asking for advice.

Daniela oversaw safety and policy at OpenAI, one of the most talked-about AI companies in the world.

But Daniela and her brother Dario - who worked there too - had misgivings over safety levels at OpenAI and thought it could be time to walk away.

So she did what many do when we can't make a decision about something, she phoned a friend. The friend's reply was direct and to the point: "You don't need to be on the phone with me. You already know the answer."

The Amodei siblings and a handful of other colleagues quit. Together, they set up a rival â Anthropic.

Six years on and Anthropic, the maker of leading AI model Claude, has predicted revenues of $52bn in 2026 and $135bn in 2027, according to analyst estimates.

It is selling shares to the public for the first time in what's known as an Initial Public Offering or IPO, aiming for a valuation of an estimated $1.4tn (Â£1tn), which would propel it straight into the top 10 of the most valuable US companies.

The sale comes at a crucial time when many are wondering whether, amid increasing concern about the potential of AI, the industry's bubble is showing signs of bursting.

Where once investors were "all in" on a technological revolution to rival the railroads, the car, electricity and the internet, some are now hedging their bets, amid arguments that AI prices will be driven down as the technology becomes more common and concerns that a hefty wedge of the AI investment is tech giants investing in each other.

Shares in companies that supply the raw computing power behind AI, like Coreweave and Nebius, have seen their value cut in half while even the mighty Nvidia has lost its crown as the world's most valuable company with its shares down 30% - a staggering $2tn fall in value.

So could the Anthropic IPO reinflate the bubble or will it be remembered as the last gasp of a boom that is heading for a bust?

At an event last week, Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei told JPMorgan Chase's CEO Jamie Dimon that his guiding principle was to "never let your company be worth a single dollar more than it's worth".

The company is selling roughly $81bn worth of shares to the general public and a further $26bn on to other big tech companies. It is comfortably the single biggest money raising event in US corporate history.

It's an old adage that during a gold rush you should buy companies that sell the picks and shovels as opposed to those staking everything on striking gold. In AI's case that's the chip makers, the data centre builders, the energy providers. But Anthropic is arguably the gold itself and this IPO will see what value people put on it, according to Fiona Cincotta from StoneX trading.

"I think the reason that this IPO is so important is basically it's the first ability for the public investors to access a pure play AI trade," she says, adding that previous offerings have been about "other parts of the ecosystem, which are, the semiconductors, the chips, the, data centres. But this is the first time that we were actually able to access the AI provider."

Daniela Amodei helped set up a rival company to OpenAI

What a company is worth depends almost entirely on predicted future earnings.

It's that calculation that many think presents the sharpest needle to the AI bubble.

Will current and future users of AI be prepared to pay enough to justify the trillions being spent on the chips, data centres, energy and employees, some of whom can command annual salaries of over $100m?

"AI is certainly in a financial bubble. While LLMs [large language models] offer genuine utility and are undoubtedly here to stay, the economics don't add up," said Gary Marcus, Professor Emeritus at New York University and author of Taming Silicon Valley, speaking earlier this year.

"[The] more companies build AI models, the [more the] technology becomes commoditised, eliminating meaningful competitive advantages and driving down prices."

So far, the growth rates in Anthropic revenue are extraordinary.

But these explosive growth rates hide a potential problem. Around a quarter of that revenue came from just a handful of companies, among them Google and Amazon.

It's also concentrated in the US which accounts for two-thirds of its revenue.

There is also the staggering network of circular financing found in the AI world. For example, Anthropic has agreements to buy or lease hundreds of billions of dollars worth of chips and computing power from a handful of companies including Broadcom, Google, Amazon. Many of those firms are also investors in Anthropic and major customers of its products.

It's not just some Wall Street investors questioning whether the AI boom is all it's cracked up to be. Out on Main Street, ordinary Americans have concerns too.

With the US driving so much of global AI growth, a growing public backlash against a technology that threatens to replace millions of jobs and hoover up vast amounts of energy and water became a factor in the recent midterm elections in the US.

Many areas in the US have passed moratoriums on data centre development and many candidates deemed too close to big tech found that association a political liability rather than an asset. Many Democrat candidates publicly opposed data centre development and won.

Added to that, the Pied Piper of AI, chip maker Nvidia's CEO Jensen Huang, resigned last week after admitting to having been "unfaithful" to his wife after reports he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, Alice Huang and another with a company executive who reported to him.

Dario Amodei is one of Silicon Valley's big beasts

Huang's spectacular fall from grace and Nvidia's 30% fall in value from its peak have left AI investors looking for a new champion for the AI boom that has underpinned the US stock market and arguably much of its entire economy for the last two years.

If Anthropic and its CEO are to be AI's new Pied Piper, he will be playing a different tune to the relentlessly upbeat, leather jacket-clad talisman Huang.

While Huang wanted AI development to continue full throttle, Anthropic was built on misgivings over the levels of safety and responsibility the OpenAI leavers felt were being exercised over the development of society-changing technology.

Amodei has warned that AI capabilities could enable biological weapon development, mass surveillance, authoritarianism and the replacement of millions of white-collar, tax-paying workers.

He has called for a slowdown in its development as its powers are growing at such an exponential rate that it could very soon elude any human oversight.

That said, his paternalistic overtures have been sceptically received by those who point out that the pace of Anthropic's development of new more powerful models has shown no sign of the slowdown he's advocating for the rest of the industry.

But for all the concerns, just as with Elon Musk's SpaceX before it, the hype and the FOMO (fear of missing out) on an undoubtedly profound moment in technological and perhaps human development, means that buying interest is high.

It's reported that orders to buy shares exceed the number on offer by over six times.

With demand outstripping supply by such a margin, a first-day jump is to be expected given the oversubscription, but it doesn't tell you much about long-run return.

Using SpaceX as an example again, shares initially rose nearly 70% from its IPO sale price of $135 to $225. Shares in SpaceX are currently back to $145.

The Anthropic IPO could be wildly successful and blow a bit of fresh new air into what some maintain still looks a lot like a bubble.

Eileen Burbidge, founder of tech investors Passion Capital, is one of those who reject the notion that AI is a bubble about to burst.

"Maybe the growth of the bubble has slowed down a little bit, but actually I wouldn't be willing to say that it's about to burst yet," says Burbidge. "I think this IPO could actually mean everyone could be off to the races again."

Fiona Cincotta believes Anthropic's IPO is about far more than just Anthropic. "I think what's going to be really interesting here is I think it can actually be used as a valuation benchmark for the entire AI ecosystem," she says.

"That's why I don't think this is just going to affect the share price of Anthropic. I think this is going to have much wider implications across the whole AI trade."

Two things can be true at the same time. The Anthropic IPO could be wildly successful - FOMO is a powerful emotion in financial markets. But its probable success won't soothe the unease in some quarters that not every company, every investor and every citizen will think of AI as the answer to humanity's problems.

#139. California targets Nvidia in China AI chip probe

California has served an investigative subpoena on the AI chip designer Nvidia in the wake of a scandal that ended in the departure of longtime CEO Jensen Huang.

The state's top law enforcement officer, attorney general Rob Bonta, said he is probing whether Nvidia or Huang's actions resulted in China obtaining advanced chips or confidential information, adding that such actions could violate California law if the company is found to have mislead investors and the public.

The BBC has contacted Nvidia for comment.

"Nvidia is in a messy, transnational situation but the underlying principle of our corporate securities law is clear: Companies cannot mislead their investors," Bonta said on Thursday.

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

Nvidia has its global headquarters in Santa Clara, California.

#138. Ford starts U.S. battery cell production using Chinese tech amid geopolitical tensions

MARSHALL, Mich. — Ford Motor has started producing battery cells in the U.S. for its next generation of electric vehicles using technologies licensed by Chinese battery giant CATL.

The automaker said Wednesday that cell production at a $3 billion plant in Marshall, Michigan — roughly 100 miles west of Detroit — started last month and the cells are now shipping out. They'll be used in prototypes for its Fathom small electric pickup truck, which CEO Jim Farley has touted as rivaling Tesla and Chinese competitors.

The production marks a milestone for the Detroit automaker and is the next step toward using lower-cost lithium iron phosphate, or LFP, battery cells in U.S. vehicles. The technology was created in the U.S. but commercialized largely in China.

The automaker's decision to make the batteries at the roughly 2 million-square-foot facility has been criticized by some lawmakers and U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy over Ford's ties to CATL.

Alan Clarke, Ford's vice president of advanced development projects, said licensing the technology from CATL helped the automaker bring the low-cost battery chemistry to the U.S. as quickly as possible.

"If we want to get to being able to produce affordable EVs in the United States, we need to also do it the fastest way possible," Clarke said Tuesday during a tour of the facility. "CATL being the biggest in the world means that they're the best supplier to get us that technology and help us learn ourselves how to produce LFP cells."

The U.S. government has increased scrutiny of Chinese ties with U.S. companies even as President Donald Trump is meeting this week with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin.

In September, Duffy sent a letter to Farley expressing "profound concern" about Ford's ties to Chinese companies that it believes could be detrimental to the Detroit carmaker and the U.S. automotive industry. The White House a day later followed up by commending the automaker for its domestic jobs and manufacturing.

Ford — the largest producer of U.S. vehicles — has routinely touted its American roots, jobs and manufacturing, especially as the Biden and Trump administrations have pushed to onshore and grow U.S. production.

Ford's battery plant in Marshall is expected to be capable of producing 40 battery cells a minute, equating to 20 gigawatt-hours annually for EVs and the company's energy storage business. That's down from initial plans of up to 40 gigawatt-hours annually amid an EV pullback by the automaker and broader industry.

Ford has said the production pace will help reduce battery costs and that the facility has room for additional capacity if needed.

The facility is highly automated, but Ford said it's still in the early stages of certifying its processes, as it plans to ramp up cell production next year before the expected launch of the Fathom pickup during the fourth quarter of 2027.

#137. A wave of 'pent-up quits' could boost turnover in the job market, expert says: 'The labor market is a bit like a coiled spring'

Frustrated workers could trigger a wave of turnover, creating a shift in the sluggish job market, according to experts at the job boards Indeed and Glassdoor.

That sluggishness, or what labor economists call a low-hire, low-fire economy, means that fewer people are getting hired, and fewer workers are leaving their jobs — even as a growing share of job holders report frustration with their career opportunities. In 2026, 21% of workers report feeling dissatisfied with their career prospects, according to Glassdoor reviewer data. Dissatisfied workers rated their jobs at a one or two out of five stars.

A stagnant job market can discourage even the unhappiest of workers from quitting, but hiring could soon ramp up. Indeed's Job Posting Index, or JPI, which tracks the level of job postings on Indeed relative to a pre-pandemic baseline, saw year-over-year growth for the first time in September 2026 since 2022. The upward trend suggests that the job market is "heating up," Glassdoor chief economist Daniel Zhao tells CNBC Make It.

As more opportunities open up, job holders might regain the confidence to exit, turning into "a wave of quits and turnover," Zhao says. Turnover could also help those who are out of a job secure employment as roles that were once filled become vacant.

"The labor market is a bit like a coiled spring right now," Zhao said at a press event Tuesday in New York City. "There's a lot of pent-up potential energy, and it's unclear exactly which way that that spring is going to release that energy."

In a healthy job market, employee dissatisfaction can forecast turnover rates. When employee dissatisfaction reached 17% in April 2022, per Glassdoor reviews, quit rates also reached their highest levels at 3%, seasonally adjusted, according to data from Bureau of Labor Statistics Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey data published by the St. Louis Federal Reserve.

However, when workers feel anxious about their future job prospects, turnover can stall. Though workers feel dissatisfied with their jobs at higher rates in September 2026 than they did in April 2022, only 2.1% of workers quit their job in the month, according to the BLS JOLTS.

"There aren't those opportunities to climb the career ranks internally or externally," Zhao says of the job market. "There's no outlet for the frustration or dissatisfaction that [workers] might be feeling."

Employee turnover was one focus of Indeed and Glassdoor's 2027 Hiring and Worklife Trends Report, published Wednesday. Their research, which assumed a consistent relationship between worker dissatisfaction and resignations based on historical data, found that the quit rate is 0.22% lower than what employee sentiment would typically suggest. While the figure seems small, it is equivalent to an additional 349,000 quits per month.

"Workers are very hesitant to quit, especially without something lined up, because hiring is very sluggish, and long-term unemployment is looming over them," Zhao tells CNBC Make It.

While unemployment levels are stable, people are remaining jobless for longer, according to data from the BLS. Those who face long-term unemployment, job seekers who have been out of work for 27 weeks or more, accounted for 27.6% of all unemployed people in October 2026.

Even if more jobs open, turnover across sectors could vary. Industries that are highly exposed to artificial intelligence like software development, engineering and banking previously dragged Indeed's JPI down, meaning employers in these sectors posted fewer jobs. Indeed's data shows that postings in these sectors have since seen growth, and could result in what experts call "pent-up quits," which occur when dissatisfied workers leave their jobs once market conditions improve. Job posts on Indeed for employers in industries with less AI exposure like healthcare have softened, according to experts.

However, business and professional services, which includes banking, shed 27,000 jobs in October 2026, according to data from ADP Research.

"As we look ahead to 2027, that increase in demand might not necessarily translate into more hires, and it also might not be enough hires to give workers the confidence that they can actually quit their jobs and move on to something better," Zhao says.

Even as the job market appears to be trending in a positive direction, further economic pressure could continue to place a strain on employers and their hiring plans.

"Nobody was really expecting 2026 to come with the geopolitical challenges that have happened and the the impact that has had on energy prices and inflation," Zhao says. "And so there's always the outside chance that something happens in 2027 that derails the job market from the path that it's on."

#134. Anthropic becomes one of world's most valuable public companies in stock market debut

Watch: How did Anthropic's stock market debut unfold?

Anthropic has become one of the most valuable public companies in the world after its shares began trading on the stock market, taking its valuation to roughly $1.76 trillion (Â£1.34 trillion).

The artificial intelligence (AI) company's Thursday debut on the Nasdaq stock exchange was a crucial test for the industry, given the huge amount of money being poured into advancing the technology.

Anthropic's shares flew past its allocation price of $83 each, showing there was plenty of excitement around AI.

Yet the company, which is behind AI chatbot Claude, will remain under intense scrutiny in the months ahead to answer the key question surrounding AI: is it all worth it?

In just a few hours of trading, Anthropic's stock seemed to show that investors remain uncertain of where exactly AI is heading. The share price rose immediately to $111.53, sending the company's market value to almost $2 trillion, before falling by the end of the day as the shares closed at $97.17.

Some investors said Anthropic's debut seemed to be a success, as shares rose roughly 17% during the day, but the broader tech market did not perform as well.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index closed down nearly 2.3%. Stocks fell in the largest tech firms, including Google's parent company Alphabet, which was down by 2.9%, while Amazon lost almost 3.9%.

The buildout happening around this era of AI is among the most expensive endeavours in the history of technology, with more than $1 trillion being spent in the last year alone by a handful of major tech companies.

The speed at which investment has grown has led to ongoing concerns of a financial bubble.

"Bubbles don't burst on the day of the largest deal," Richard Whittle, an economist with the University of Salford said. "They burst later, if the positive future becomes suspect."

Anthropic has made one of the fastest trips from start up to IPO in history

While the basics of the technology have been around for decades, the more recent advent of more powerful computers and related components have allowed the processing of more data than ever.

The result has been AI chatbots that simulate human speech and operate tasks like software coding, digital image generation, and searching the internet more quickly.

It represents a new way of using computers that has led to the rapid rise of Anthropic, which started just five years ago and is best known as the maker of the popular AI chatbot Claude.

As the first purely AI company to go public, Anthropic will almost certainly see significant swings in price on the stock market going forward, according to Ben Barringer, who leads technology research at Quilter Cheviot.

The "initial exuberance" will wear off, Barringer said, pointing to what happened with SpaceX earlier this year.

That company, led by Elon Musk, saw shares jump by about 10% on the first day of trading, taking the company to a record-breaking valuation of $2.2 trillion.

Within a month shares had fallen below their debut price, and have mostly fallen further since.

Most tech companies see their shares fall after hitting the stock market, often after reporting quarterly results for the first time.

But many such companies, including Meta, Amazon and Google, have gone on to become the largest and most profitable corporations in the world.

With Anthropic, investors are not only looking at a notable tech company with a major valuation, and a potentially big future in business, but a company that is the first of its kind on the stock market.

"There's no obvious peer to compare it to," Neil Wilson, an investor strategist at Saxo UK, said.

Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

Daniela Amodei: Anthropic and the price of AI

So, as individual investors and even researchers and investors at large funds ask themselves if Anthropic is really worth more than $1.7 trillion, or even more, there will be few answers available.

"We'll have a stab at it," Wilson said. "I don't think anyone really knows."

A few things will likely impact the company's success on the stock market in the near-term.

One of those things is the prospect of new laws governing the use of AI tools. In the US, where Anthropic is based, Democrats will be in many more positions of power come 2027, and several politicians have already said they will be looking to ensure AI tools are safe and have some guardrails attached.

Such regulation is currently unlikely at the federal level. US President Donald Trump on Thursday spoke about AI during the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in China, alongside Chinese and Russian counterparts, Xi Jinping and Vladimir Putin.

Trump reiterated his position against "any form of robust regulation" around the technology, adding, "we won't allow that to happen, frankly."

The same day, however, a bipartisan group of US lawmakers formed the House AI Safety and Innovation Caucus, which will work specifically on AI regulation and safety considerations.

A significant driver of this kind of scrutiny is Anthropic itself. Company executives, including co-founder and CEO Dario Amodei, have been outspoken in their purported fears that future AI tools will become so capable they will outsmart humans, to the point where extinction is possible.

Anthropic has always put itself forward in public as an AI company that takes the safety of its technology very seriously.

Daniela Amodei, Anthropic's president and sister of Dario Amodei, boasted on Thursday that the company "had an AI safety team before we launched our first product".

Despite the company's claims that the technology it is building at a rapid pace could be dangerous, Anthropic has made one of the quickest trips from start-up to IPO in history.

Anthropic raises $81bn in record-breaking stock market debut

#131. Tens of thousands of UK fridges secured from hackers in secret nationwide operation

Sweden was among countries to report a cyber incident in which some supermarket fridge units had been hiked up in temperature

A behind the scenes cyber-security operation has taken place to secure tens of thousands of fridges and freezers at major UK supermarkets, warehouses, restaurants, petrol stations and stadiums from hackers, the BBC has learned.

Recent waves of cyber-attacks in Europe have seen tonnes of food spoiled at hundreds of stores, causing millions of pounds of damage.

France has blamed Iran for one of the waves of attacks that impacted its supermarkets, a claim Tehran denies.

It was thought the UK had largely escaped such hacks as the British refrigeration industry relies on different devices but there was in fact a potential vulnerability the sector has been rushing to fix.

In late October, security researchers discovered a major flaw that, if found by hackers, could have led to even worse damage to the UK.

Since then, researchers, IT teams and the National Cyber Security Centre have been working around the clock to warn dozens of companies.

The BBC has not reported on the large-scale, secretive operation until now to reduce the chances of hackers causing harm if they found the vulnerability.

Tech Life: The everyday tech at risk of hacks

As Europe sees a wave of cyber-attacks on commercial fridges, we investigate how everyday devices are vulnerable to basic attacks using easily-available tools.

The security threat was discovered by staff at refrigeration consultants Omega Solutions - it was then verified and expanded upon by experts at Pen Test Partners who helped raise the alarm.

The National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC), the Department for Environment, Food & Rural Affairs (Defra) and Food Standards Agency (FSA) were then involved in the campaign to plug the security holes.

They held industry conference calls and tasked teams to contact firms at risk.

Thousands of devices controlling tens of thousands of refrigeration devices were taken off the open internet either remotely or, in some cases, physically - with staff ordered to remove internet cables and monitor temperatures manually.

BBC news has learnt most major supermarkets have been forced to divert IT teams to address the risks, with a worker at one large chain saying they took hundreds of devices offline every day over the course of a week.

"It did feel like a race against time as we were expecting another wave of these fridge hacks to come," said the person, who did not want to be publicly named.

"From a business perspective it could have been potentially devastating with business interruption and supply chain problems. Our warehouses have millions of pounds worth of food that could have been spoiled if any hackers found out about this method of attack," they added.

There have been three waves of hacks on refrigeration temperature controllers so far this year:

In August, fridges at a small number of US military bases were impacted

On 11 and 12 October hundreds of supermarkets and food processing sites in France were hit when Danfoss temperature control systems were targeted

On 1 and 2 November dozens of supermarkets across seven European countries were hit, with Belgium and Luxembourg the worst affected. A small number of blood bank sites in France were also impacted

The waves of attacks prompted an expert at Omega Solutions to examine the security of the most popular refrigeration controllers in the UK, made by British firm Resource Data Management (RDM).

RDM disputes the critical nature of the vulnerabilities exposed but has been praised for its professional response to address the concerns, the BBC understands.

As well as supermarkets, multiple other areas of UK food and beverage industry were also warned to take action about the risks.

These include convenience stores, petrol stations, restaurants and fast food chains, sports arenas, hotels, food processing plants and cold storage sites.

Each RDM controller can be used to remotely control the temperature of dozens of fridges and freezers

"These popular controllers had a similar weak password problem to the Danfoss and other controllers that were attacked so successfully in previous hacks so we had to get the message out as soon as possible and keep it secret," said Ken Munro of Pen Test Partners.

"There is so much extra attention on these ubiquitous bits of kit now that it was likely a matter of days until criminals or nation state hackers figured it out," he added.

RDM, though, says comparisons with the units attacked in mainland Europe are not fair because hacking their fridges and freezers would have involved getting hold of a piece of hardware.

The ethical hackers say this would have been achievable as the hardware in question can be purchased cheaply online on sites like eBay.

"This was a vulnerability identified in a test environment," said Ged Evans, CEO of RDM.

He said the test "went beyond" what such exercises usually involved, as it "included dismantling our hardware".

"In any event we have acted at pace to address this issue and have already deployed first patch releases and have been in proactive contact with our customers to flag any issues and recommend mitigatory steps," he added.

RDM confirmed that there was no sign of any malicious activity on the controllers and that it continued to work with the NCSC and other authorities to monitor the situation.

What are your thoughts on this topic? Let us know

UK meat industry hit by cyber-attack which targeted European supermarket freezers

If hackers can take control of a supermarket freezer, how vulnerable is other tech?

#128. Anthropic raises $93.5 billion total as underwriters scoop up ‘greenshoe’ overallotment

Anthropic underwriters have officially exercised their overallotment of shares in the historic initial public offering, bringing the total raised to $93.5 billion, according to a release Friday.

The artificial intelligence company raised $81.3 billion on Wednesday and netted an additional $12.2 billion through the overallotment, surpassing SpaceX, which held a record IPO in June.

Anthropic said it sold an aggregate of 1,127,000,000 shares of its Class A common stock at $83 per share in the offering. It sold 980 million shares on Wednesday and its underwriters had the option to purchase up to an additional 147 million shares by Friday as part of the overallotment, which is commonly referred to as the "greenshoe."

Shares of the Claude maker opened trading at $111.50 on the Nasdaq on Thursday, but slid throughout the day and closed at $97.17. The stock sank 12% in premarket trading on Friday during a broad tech sell-off.

In addition to the sale of its Class A common stock, Anthropic closed a concurrent sale of its Class N common stock after a group of tech companies, including Advanced Micro Devices, Google, Microsoft, Nvidia and others, agreed to participate in a $26 billion private placement at the IPO price. Anthropic said Friday that aggregate gross proceeds from its IPO, including the private placement, totaled $119.5 billion, before deducting offering expenses.

Anthropic dodged an unforgiving series of obstacles on its road to the Nasdaq on Thursday, as investors brooded over Jensen Huang's shocking ouster at Nvidia, voters' stinging rebuke of the tech during the midterms and whether rising interest rates would stymie industry plans for a multitrillion-dollar AI infrastructure buildout.

CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the company in 2021 after he and a handful of other researchers, including his sister Daniela, quit their jobs at OpenAI, now Anthropic's chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, which have captivated the corporate world and fueled the company's historic rise.

The siblings celebrated the IPO as a "milestone" in an interview with CNBC on Thursday, but said there's more work ahead for Anthropic.

"For us, it's like one stop on the journey," Daniela Amodei said. "We think about this as, 'Wow, this is an incredible moment for Anthropic for our mission, but we're going to wake up tomorrow and we're going to be the same company.'"

#127. Mouldy, infested housing for vulnerable people sold as ethical investments abroad

Many of the companies marketing supported housing investments were linked to developer Lee Jones

"This building is hell on Earth," says Phil, describing the black mould and rats at Castleview House, the block of flats in Runcorn, Cheshire, where he lives.

But to an estate agent called BritskÃ© Reality - which translates as British Real Estate - working with investors hundreds of miles away in the Czech Republic, the flat Phil rents through his benefits was an exciting business opportunity.

The estate agent's marketing material said the flat was a government-funded source of income for investors, where tenants were taken care of and the property was in excellent condition.

A Czech investor bought it last year for Â£163,150, more than double the Â£75,000 cost of similar apartments in the block.

The BBC has found more than 40 companies marketing similar properties to individuals in the UK and abroad, as businesses seek to profit from a government system intended to provide housing and support for vulnerable people.

About a dozen of these companies work with a property developer called Lee Jones, whose company sold Phil's flat.

The homes they are selling to investors are advertised as supported accommodation, a type of housing for people with issues including mental health problems or drug abuse issues, as well as people fleeing domestic violence.

The BBC saw mould on ceilings and overflowing rubbish when we visited Castleview House

Providers offering what is known as supported exempt accommodation are allowed to charge the taxpayer more than the usual housing benefit cap, because they have higher running costs. Tenants are entitled to care, support and supervision - although how much support should be provided is not defined.

While there are legitimate providers helping people back into independent living, the BBC has previously reported on how some are exploiting the system by taking the higher housing benefit payments but failing to provide support.

Our latest investigation found companies marketing supported housing properties to people in Hong Kong, Macau, Nigeria, Turkey, the Netherlands, the UK and other countries as a "secure", "government-backed" investment.

We also found adverts made misleading promises about the quality of the accommodation and support, with tenants saying they were receiving little or no care, while we observed examples of poor quality, unsafe accommodation.

Housing charities and experts have told the BBC the sector has been transformed into a profit-generating machine at the expense of some tenants' safety, care, and dignity.

Labour MP Abena Oppong-Asare, who chairs the Housing Select Committee, said it was "outrageous" that supported housing properties were being marketed as investments "particularly where there's public funds involved".

The BBC's findings were "alarming" and showed a sector "open to abuse", she added.

Promotional materials such as this social media post advertise the homes as "Secure, Government-Backed Wealth"

Lee Jones's company The Investment Room converted Castleview House in 2019 from an office block into more than 200 residential flats, including the one where Phil now lives.

Then Jones partnered with Nurture Housing Association. His company granted Nurture a 25-year-lease on some of the flats, to provide housing for people on government benefits. Jones has a close business relationship with Nurture's chief executive Chris Smith, with the pair also acting as joint directors of other housing companies.

Jones then worked with estate agents, which marketed the housing to investors in the UK and overseas using the regular cashflow generated by the 25-year lease with Nurture and the promise of government funding to justify inflated prices.

Lee Jones and Nurture have repeated this with property developments across England, including in Doncaster, Rotherham, Stockton-on-Tees and Bradford. These type of properties are also in Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland but are funded and regulated differently.

Because neither profit-making private companies nor individuals can act as supported housing providers themselves, co-operation between developers, investors and publicly funded providers has become common.

The Czech marketing material found by the BBC said Nurture provides housing for people with mental health issues who receive the "aftercare program pursuant to Section 117 of the Mental Health Act", meaning they have been sectioned and discharged from hospital with a detailed care plan.

But Phil says he has never been sectioned under the Mental Health Act.

He does have severe depression and anxiety, and he is currently out of work. But he says he has never had any support or care, or met anyone working for Nurture during his time at the flat.

"Whoever marketed this property, the way they've worded it is total dishonesty," says Phil. "If they're looking to make as much profit as they can, then they clearly have no plans to fix the issues in the building."

Marketing images appeared to show Castleview House as newly refurbished with bright white interiors

Phil had no idea the flat he rents had been sold to overseas investors in May 2025 until the BBC contacted him. But there were clues: bills addressed to someone called Jan HÃ¡la had been coming through the letterbox for months.

Lee Jones told us that the properties his companies provide are "good quality accommodation" and he has no responsibility for the communal areas of Castleview House since he sold the freehold in 2021. He denied his relationship with Chris Smith amounted to a conflict of interest, but did not respond to questions about his role in marketing the properties.

Jan HÃ¡la, the Czech investor who now owns Phil's flat, and the BritskÃ© Reality estate agency did not respond to requests for comment.

We spoke to several other people living in flats run by Nurture Housing Association in Castleview House who said they were in supported accommodation. They reported receiving little or no help from Nurture but were afraid to speak out publicly for fear of eviction and becoming homeless.

There is also evidence that marketing materials for investors buying flats in Castleview were misleading. Investors were told Nurture was providing "specialist supported housing" - a claim which is repeated on its website.

According to the Regulator of Social Housing, this would mean it was providing a high level of care, equivalent to living in a care home.

But Nurture had not been registered as a specialised supported housing provider, the regulator said, and its claim to be providing this was "shocking".

In a statement, Nurture acknowledged that Castleview House is managed under a "general housing management model" and does not form part of its supported housing portfolio. It said it provided supported housing elsewhere through partners which were registered providers.

It said the Czech advert found by the BBC related to "historic marketing material" produced by third parties.

However, the BBC has seen marketing material from several other agents selling flats in Castleview House to investors, which also claim that Nurture is providing supported housing to the residents on a 25-year lease.

Nurture said it is not directly involved with the companies marketing the properties. But it said: "We will be reviewing communications with relevant agents and reminding them of the importance of ensuring that any information relating to Nurture is accurate, transparent and does not create misunderstanding regarding the accommodation or services provided."

While there are doubts about what kind of housing Nurture has been providing, the BBC also found other flats sold to investors overseas with tenants who were in supported exempt accommodation.

The web of companies involved in providing this housing and cross-border ownership means it can be difficult for vulnerable tenants to get help when there are problems with properties.

"The way that the current system works is there's different levels of involvement by different organisations and companies and no-one is directly accountable," Abena Oppong-Asare, the housing committee chairwoman, told us.

Mo, who has mental health issues, was housed in a studio flat in Cricklewood, north London, after he became homeless. The converted house containing his flat was bought by Gemelli London 2 Limited, a company run by French nationals living in the Czech Republic, for Â£1.83m in January last year.

Mo (right) pictured with a friend who also lived in the same building, says his ceiling collapsed and was not fixed for months

He says his supported housing provider, Windrush Alliance, receives nearly Â£2,000 a month via Barnet Council for the flat. Mo says he receives Â£340 in universal credit for living expenses every month - but he is expected to pay Windrush a monthly Â£120 service charge out of that.

Last year his ceiling collapsed after a leak went unrepaired for more than a year and Mo was temporarily housed in another flat in the property.

Mo says his weekly visits from a Windrush support worker feel like a tick-box exercise and do not help him with the mental health issues which have been keeping him out of work.

Gemelli's director Stephane Boussier told the BBC that when the company bought the property "no complaints from residents were disclosed". It was made aware of issues in August 2025, he said, and since then has "raised them with Windrush in writing, inspected the property and pressed for them to be resolved".

He added that Gemelli London 2 Limited receives about Â£1,365 per flat per month from Windrush and does not receive any of the extra housing benefit that would be used for vulnerable tenants.

Windrush said it was aware of issues at Mo's property and was "working with the building owner to put them right". According to Gemelli, the ceiling was repaired after the BBC contacted the two companies.

Windrush has previously been criticised by the Regulator for Social Housing for a "failure to meet the standards" of the regulator, including failing to ensure "tenants are safe in their homes".

It acknowledged that it "fell short in the past". It wrote off Â£1.8m in "funds to former directors and companies under their control", saying the company's financial position had been "compromised" by their actions. The company now has a new board in place.

Windrush told us its "priority is residents, not investors" and the rates it charges "are agreed with the relevant local authorities" and "any surplus is reinvested in our homes and services".

In Bradford, the BBC visited City Exchange and Colonnade House, two former office blocks which Lee Jones has converted into housing - with Nurture Housing Association managing many of the flats.

Many of the properties there were advertised as supported housing investments. Residents showed us cockroach infestations, faulty fire alarms, broken lifts, leaks through the ceiling and black mould.

Nurture Housing said it was only responsible for the individual homes that it manages and does not manage communal areas or "wider building infrastructure".

"Nurture Housing and its managing agents regularly raise these concerns with the block manager," the company said.

The freeholder for Phil's block, Castleview House, and City Exchange, blamed "defects" by the developer Lee Jones for the problems with the buildings and said it was taking legal action. Colonnade House has had several building management companies in recent years and the latest said it was addressing the problems.

None of these companies or individuals are breaking the law, but critics say they are exploiting a lack of regulation in the sector.

Three years after the Supported Housing Act was passed in England, which would put in place a licensing regime for supported housing providers and give local councils more powers to shut down providers that failed to meet the standards, it still has not been implemented.

"It's taken too long," said Jasmine Basran, head of policy and campaigns at housing charity Crisis. "In the meantime, this an area where we've seen the worst kind of exploitation."

Additional reporting by Lorna Acquah and Abi Smitton

#125. GTA 6 drives spike in download demand, as estimated sales top $2.3bn

Grand Theft Auto 6 is estimated to be the biggest entertainment launch in history after the blockbuster video game debuted on Thursday, following a 13-year wait.

Internet infrastructure firm Cloudflare said PS5 downloads in the UK peaked at 8.6 times normal levels around its launch. New Zealand, where GTA 6 was first available, saw a 14.8-fold spike four hours before, suggesting some players switched regions for early access.

Pre-orders and first-day sales totalled more than 24.5 million units, making about $2.36bn (Â£1.8bn), according to analytics site Sensor Tower.

In comparison, this year's highest-grossing movie so far, Spider-Man: Brand New Day, made $2.5bn over its entire run in cinemas.

GTA 6's maker Rockstar and parent company Take-Two have not released details of the game's sales but said demand has been "unprecedented". The BBC has contacted both firms for comment.

In its initial findings since the game's launch, Cloudflare also recorded a huge surge in traffic to Rockstar's online services, peaking at 11.2 times normal levels in Australia and 9.3 times in the US East Coast.

In Europe, UK interest was particularly strong, reaching seven times normal levels compared with 5.3 times in France and Germany.

The game is the latest instalment in Rockstar's nearly three decade old crime simulator franchise, which has become one of the most popular games in history.

At its core, the series is a satirical take on the American dream, putting players in the shoes of career criminals in Leonida, the game's Florida-inspired state.

The latest release follows new characters Lucia and her partner Jason, a pair of small-time criminals.

GTA 6 is estimated to have cost more than $1bn to develop, which would make it the most expensive video game in history.

GTA 6 gets 'near perfect' reviews as game launches

Sensor Tower said GTA 6 will be so popular that it will reduce the time gamers spend on other single-player titles by 10%.

The research firm said its estimates are based on email receipts data and other statistical sources that track millions of people daily. They also track public profiles across major gaming platforms like PlayStation and Xbox, where GTA 6 is available.

Around three quarters of the game's sales have been for the PlayStation 5 and the balance on Xbox, Sensor Tower said.

Niko Partners, a gaming market research firm, said it expects GTA 6 to make more than $3bn in sales worldwide this year.

That figure would rank GTA 6 among the highest-grossing entertainment releases ever, but it would still have some way to go to catch 1939's Gone with the Wind, which has made an estimated $4.5bn, adjusted for inflation.

Rockstar is expected to make even more money from GTA 6 over the coming years.

The New York-based studio has yet to announce when the game will be released for PCs or given a date for its multiplayer expansion, which will allow players to interact and go on missions with others over the internet.

If 2013's GTA 5 is any guide, multiplayer will add dozens more hours of gameplay and bring in even more revenue for Rockstar as players spend real money on in-game property, cars and other perks.

GTA 6 launched around the world on 19 November

Anticipation for the blockbuster title has grown through repeated delays, following a 13-year wait.

GTA 6 has been hailed by some reviewers as one of the most impressive gaming experiences ever. Entertainment media site IGN described it as an "uncompromisingly engineered" masterpiece.

The release of GTA 6 at midnight on Thursday across various time zones saw many gamers stay up through the night, while some took the following day off to play.

Its launch broke Virgin Media O2's evening traffic record as people rushed to install GTA 6's huge 212GB file on consoles.

The game's graphic content has resulted in censorship in Japan and, like earlier GTA releases, is not approved for sale in mainland China.

Has GTA 6 lived up to the hype? Have you taken time off to play?

#123. Investigators make progress in hunt for cause of FAA ground stop

Engineers and analysts searching for the root cause of the technical issues behind Friday's nationwide ground stop by the Federal Aviation Administration have been able to replicate the situation that was overwhelming parts of the air traffic system, people familiar with the investigation told CNBC.

While replicating the issue in a lab in Florida is progress, those investigating the matter have yet to determine exactly why two routers in question failed, according to the people, who requested anonymity to talk about an ongoing situation. Finding the root cause is critical to the FAA determining if there may be an issue with other routers that form the backbone of communications for the national air transportation network.

The ground stop lasted for more than four hours Friday, marking the longest halt to U.S. flight departures since 9/11.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy and FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford have said there is no indication the routers failed due to a cyberattack.

The incident in question happened shortly after L3Harris Technologies, the primary contractor supporting the FAA telecommunications network, installed a new router at a facility in El Dorado, Arkansas.

L3Harris said that it, in partnership with other tech companies, has completed more than 70% of the planned airspace communications conversion from legacy copper wires to a fiber-optic system.

"We are supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the Nov. 13 ground stop of our nation's air travel," the statement said. "L3Harris takes the nationwide ground stop and its impact on travelers, airlines and air traffic operations seriously."

Investigators said within eight minutes of its installation, the router began to transmit too much data within the FAA network.

That surge of data, 1,000 connection attempts every four to five seconds, destabilized aircraft communications and radar feeds, Duffy said.

Since the ground stop, Duffy has increased the amount of money he wants to upgrade the national air traffic system. After originally asking for $30 billion to build a digital network that will incorporate artificial intelligence, Duffy has now asked Congress for $50 billion.

#121. Airbnb's divisive new house rules come into force

New rules on what Airbnb hosts can reasonably ask guests to do have come into force.

Hosts should now not normally ask guests to wash up, for example, but can appeal to Airbnb if they have a compelling reason.

Some guests have found lists of house rules laborious to complete, with one telling the BBC "it was not relaxing at all".

But some hosts have said that the costs of paying for extra cleaning may have to be passed on to customers.

The new Airbnb rules, announced last month, allow hosts to choose from seven standard house rules, which include determining how many people can stay overnight, and whether or not pets or smoking are allowed.

Hosts can then set additional rules, but guests shouldn't be asked to do the washing up, remove sheets from beds, take rubbish out or avoid using utilities.

Airbnb has begun reviewing listings and will be sending emails to hosts, external "who need to take action" it said.

Hosts can appeal to Airbnb to argue an additional house rule is necessary, and ask Airbnb to approve it.

If the house rule is not approved and the host keeps it anyway, Airbnb will flag this to guests on the host's listing page with a message such as "includes cleaning tasks".

Airbnb said chores can make customers hesitate about booking through the platform, arguing that making house rules "clearer" would increase the number of clients.

But some hosts have said they may have to pass the extra cleaning costs on to guests.

Lisa Clewes, who runs a Welsh holiday let, told the BBC she was "horrified" at the state her guests left the house after a dinner party.

There were broken glasses, drinks cans on the floor, pans full of food on the table and fish guts left in the garden.

Clewes said it typically takes cleaners four hours to turn around her three-bedroom property.

She said guests leaving more mess could mean hiring extra cleaners and increasing costs â expenses that may ultimately be passed on to her customers.

Airbnb host Lisa Clewes said her Welsh holiday let was left 'a complete mess' by a group of guests

Edinburgh-based holiday home owner Fiona Venter told the BBC that Airbnb's new rules were "nonsense" and would not accept bookings if guests refuse to take their rubbish away.

Talking about a time when she was worried she might get a mice infestation after six bags of rubbish were left in her cottage, she said: "I would rather have my house still standing."

But Fiona said increased cleaning costs due to the rule change would be unfair to those who do clean up after themselves: "Why should they pay extra for the guests that choose to abuse a cottage?"

Airbnb hosts can no longer make guests strip beds and wash dishes under new rules

No more washing up: How Airbnb's new house rules have divided hosts and guests

The changes have come as a relief to guests like Jessica Newhook, who says she has been faced with lengthy checkout lists, often on top of hefty cleaning fees.

She booked an Airbnb in Florida for a family reunion of 11 guests and says they had to "leave the property move-in ready for the cleaners", despite paying a $250 (Â£187) cleaning fee.

"We had rules about everything and it was not relaxing at all," she said.

The new rules have added to growing frustration among owners following increases in Airbnb's commission rates last year.

Many are investing in their own websites and social media to attract direct bookings and avoid platform fees.

Not all hosts are concerned about the changes, however.

Matt Porsz, who hosts and manages other Airbnb properties full-time, told the BBC previously the review system encourages both hosts and guests to behave reasonably.

#119. Should AI be judging our credit worthiness?

Sophie Hope's credit score was hit by identity fraud

Getting approval for a mortgage, a car loan or even a mobile phone contract can rest on a credit score.

Sophie Hope was on the sharp end of just such a decision.

"When I first checked my credit score, it was excellent. But then my mortgage application was suddenly denied by the lender because of my credit report. It was a massive shock as I'd never had bad credit. When I looked into it, my score had gone to 'very poor'," she says.

It turns out, the Brighton-based nail technician and content creator, had been the victim of identity fraud.

"Someone had opened accounts in my name across various catalogue sites and ordered multiple items, including a PlayStation 4 and Nike socks," she says.

"Without me knowing, the debt was transferred to a secondary finance company. It spent three to four years accumulating in the background. It was only when I went to apply for a mortgage that I discovered how severely it had dragged down my credit score," she says.

Credit scores are "backward looking" says Funmi Olufunwa

Hope's experience shows how crucial a credit score can be, but, as in her case, it does not tell the full story.

"As a nation, we are overly obsessed with credit scores," says Funmi Olufunwa, a consumer finance lawyer and founder of financial education business, Hoops Finance.

Three firms - Experian, Equifax and TransUnion - produce credit scores in the UK, and they all work from roughly the same data.

"A credit score is backward looking. It's looking at what has happened in the past, which doesn't necessarily predict the future," says Olufunwa.

"But when somebody's looking to lend you money, they're looking at what actually is affordable," she says.

Traditional credit scoring systems also disadvantage "thin-file applicants" - young people and recent immigrants to the UK who won't have built up a credit history.

But with more financial data available than ever before - and a growing appetite for AI to make sense of it - could artificial intelligence help lenders work out who is safe to lend to?

In the US lending decisions will often hinge on a credit score from a company called FICO, which uses data from the big three credit assessment firms.

But AI-powered lending firms, Upstart and Zest AI have both built businesses which offer lenders an extra option when it comes to assessing a client's credit worthiness.

"Thanks to our machine-learning models that look at more than 2,500 variables, we believe we can make significantly more loans at low prices to borrowers from every demographic group," Upstart said in statement.

Mortgage expert and founder and CEO of The Mortgage Mum, Sarah Tucker says: "AI has the potential to help lenders analyse much broader sets of information, identify patterns more quickly and build a more individual picture of someone's affordability and risk."

If technology allows lenders to understand so-called thin-file applicants better, rather than simply applying increasingly rigid rules, Tucker believes there is "a real opportunity to improve financial inclusion".

But like with many other applications of AI into everyday life, there are concerns.

"Consumers need to understand what information is being used to make decisions about them, and they need to be able to challenge something when it is wrong," Tucker explains.

Credit scores have been a contentious topic for decades for this very reason, says Sandra Wachter, professor of Technology and Regulation at the University of Oxford.

"We are not assessing if past decisions were right," Wachter argues, adding, AI does not know about existing social inequalities that impact decisions and could just "replicate what it has seen in the past".

While companies like Upstart and Zest AI see technology as a tool for financial inclusion, Wachter believes that real transformation requires accountability, as well as automation.

She stresses that firms must apply existing regulations and establish new guardrails to ensure that AI does not simply perpetuate the biases of the past.

"Not putting any safeguards in place will fortify, foster, and enhance discrimination."

"We've invested in creating objective, automated evaluations to identify bias, and we've incorporated those evaluations into our model training process so it evolves to become even more accurate and more fair over time," the company said in a statement.

Evangeline Atkinson (left) and Laura Mills co-founders of Noggin HQ

Evangeline Atkinson had a frustrating experience over her credit score.

"I was at university, paying rent, paying bills, as I had been doing for many years. I tried to get a Â£30-a-month phone contract, and they said no," she recalls.

"When I dug into it, it was because I was completely invisible to the system. No record could be found for me at any of the big three credit reference agencies."

That experience inspired her to try to change the system.

In 2021, Atkinson founded a credit reference agency with her best-friend from school, who had also experienced difficulties getting credit.

Their firm, Noggin HQ, was authorised by the FCA in June. Atkinson explains it was set up to help consumers access affordable credit by analysing their current account transactional data.

The platform helps lenders interpret thousands of individual data points about a person's spending habits.

Crucially, it does this without using AI, which Atkinson has also argued "carries the risk of inbuilt bias".

"You have to be very careful", she warns. "It's very sensitive data and it would be very easy for the models to be inferring various kind of protected characteristics."

Experian announced a partnership with OpenAI in August

Perhaps conscious of change, the big three credit scoring firms have all started experimenting with AI.

Experian announced a partnership with OpenAI in August 2026, letting users check their credit score inside ChatGPT. Equifax launched a platform called Equifax Amplify AI, which it says uses machine learning to sharpen credit assessment and fraud detection.

Meanwhile, in March TransUnion announced it was integrating Google's Gemini models "within its proprietary analytic data assets".

As for the future of credit scoring, companies are starting to look beyond traditional methods, according to consumer finance lawyer Funmi Olufunwa.

"They are beginning to realise that traditional credit scoring is inherently backward-looking, and that there are better predictors of the future out there."

Whether consumers will be happy with AI playing a part in working out their future credit ratings is another thing.

Any change might be led by the younger, tech-savvy generation.

"They feel comfortable with their data being used this way, simply because it's the world they've grown up in.

"I wonder if older cohorts, who may not use technology as much, will be quite as comfortable with it," says Olufunwa.

X-planes: Are they needed in the new era of drones?

The start-ups hoping to return battery making to the US

#118. Trump says he discussed Jensen Huang with Xi, apparently contradicting Bessent

President Donald Trump on Friday said he and Chinese leader Xi Jinping discussed their shared affinity for former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang this week, appearing to contradict Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent who earlier in the day said the ousted leader was not mentioned in the Trump administration's latest talks with China in Shenzhen.

"I did discuss that with President Xi, and he said, 'Oh, brilliant, brilliant! He's a brilliant man.' He is," Trump said at a White House press conference.

Asked earlier Friday on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" if Huang, a close ally of Trump's, had come up in those talks, Bessent said "no," without elaborating.

The Treasury Department did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment on the apparent discrepancy between Trump and Bessent.

Trump also reiterated that he would like to see Huang — who was replaced by interim CEO Colette Kress after resigning amid allegations of an extramarital affair with a Chinese spy — be restored as Nvidia's CEO.

"I would love to see Jensen be given a second chance," Trump said. "You know, we're a country of second chances, and I'd love to be able to see him. That's a very important company to us, and he put us in a position where we are literally 10 years advanced over other companies."

Nvidia's board is not considering bringing Huang back, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC earlier this week.

The president's comments came amid growing scrutiny of advanced U.S. chips, including those made by Nvidia, ending up in China either legally or by being diverted via third countries.

Both Trump's and Bessent's remarks are potentially an indication that the Trump administration may not focus on cracking down on China's efforts to acquire advanced chips.

Bessent suggested that U.S. policy allowing the sale of Nvidia's powerful H200 AI chips to China remains unchanged following the talks in Shenzhen this week.

"We've already agreed that the ... H200s are the threshold level of chips that could be sold to the Chinese, and, you know, that continues as planned," Bessent said.

"I wouldn't anticipate any change in the policy," he added after CNBC's Sara Eisen noted that Nvidia said it had stopped selling H200s to China.

The administration ignoring the subject of Huang's affair with a possible Chinese spy — whose Singapore-based company Megaspeed has been accused of acting as a cutout for Beijing — contrasts with the importance Trump has placed on beating China in the race to artificial intelligence dominance. It could suggest that despite his support for the U.S. AI industry, Trump is less concerned with addressing China's ongoing efforts to acquire U.S. semiconductors.

Trump has also defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who has come under fire from multiple directions for reportedly slow-walking export restrictions on Chinese chip sales. "I understand both sides of it," Trump said on Monday when he also said he's like to see Huang back as CEO.

Bessent's appearance on CNBC came after Trump's return from a tour through Asia that saw him meet with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen, China.

The meeting was historic as the first time the leaders of the three countries had met without other nations' counterparts. But the trilateral summit appeared to yield few deliverables on any number of the most pressing global issues related to the countries, including the Ukraine and Iran wars or the ongoing AI race.

Bessent told CNBC on Friday that there was a "robust discussion" between Trump and Xi on trade policy and AI.

They "agreed that humans should always be involved in any military decisions that SI makes, and the SI should not be allowed to be autonomous for major military actions," he said, abbreviating the term "super intelligence," Trump's attempted rebrand of AI.

In a CNBC appearance last week, Bessent lamented the "leakage" of U.S.-made AI chips to China and blamed the Commerce Department, led by Lutnick, for slow-walking export controls on suspected Chinese cutouts like Megaspeed.

Multiple Trump administration leaders have long sought to impose export restrictions on Megaspeed, but Commerce "has the pen" and "you will have to ask them about their internal processes," Bessent said in that interview.

On Friday, however, Bessent was much more tight-lipped.

Asked if there is a dispute between him and Lutnick over how aggressively the U.S. should be pursuing Chinese export controls, Bessent said, "I think we're all committed to following President Trump's instructions."

"I think we are going to see that the ... proper chip restrictions are in place, and the remote access has been curtailed," he added. Commerce recently took steps to close one of China's paths to accessing AI processors.

Correction: Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent spoke to CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." A previous version of this story misstated the name of the show.

#116. Walmart hikes outlook as CEO tells CNBC retailer aims to keep prices low despite soaring fuel costs

Walmart on Thursday reported earnings and revenue that narrowly topped estimates and raised its full-year outlook as it saw strong e-commerce growth ahead of the busy holiday season.

Walmart said global e-commerce sales surged 17% in the fiscal third quarter, driven by store-fulfilled pickup and delivery, with Walmart U.S. up 23% and Sam's Club U.S. rising 30%. Its Walmart U.S. advertising business saw a 37% sales increase for the quarter.

Walmart said it rolled back prices on nearly 8,000 products across its stores, a focus as U.S. consumers worry about the cost of living in the face of elevated food and gas prices.

CEO John Furner told CNBC's Becky Quick that the company has seen higher-than-expected diesel costs, but that tariff refunds have helped the retailer to offset those inputs and maintain lower prices.

"The reality is that increases in diesel fuel and transportation, they do work their way into cost of goods," Furner said in an exclusive interview. "So we're hoping to see these costs come down as soon as possible, and we'll keep prices as low as we can."

CFO John David Rainey told CNBC that growth in online orders for Walmart and Sam's Club was driven by an emphasis on speed of delivery and reduced prices. He added that Walmart saw its largest share gains in the food category in three years during the third quarter.

"This was another really solid quarter for us, we feel good about it," Rainey told CNBC. "We continue to gain share while continuing to improve the profitability of our digital businesses."

Walmart also raised its full-year guidance, now expecting net sales to increase between 4.9% and 5.2%, compared with previous guidance of a 4% to 5% increase. Rainey said Walmart is maintaining "flexibility" in its guidance.

Walmart anticipates adjusted earnings per share will come in between $2.84 and $2.89, compared with a prior range of $2.80 to $2.87. It's also now expecting adjusted operating income to increase between 8% and 8.6%, up from the previous outlook of between 7% and 8.5% growth.

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter ended Oct. 31, the company reported net income of $4.95 billion, or 62 cents per share, down from $6.14 billion, or 77 cents per share, a year earlier. Revenue rose 5.2%.

Adjusting for investment gains and losses, tax matters and reorganization charges, Walmart earned 65 cents per share.

Walmart U.S. recorded net sales of $125 billion, up 3.6% from a year earlier. Its comparable sales excluding fuel rose 2.9%, in line with Wall Street expectations, according to StreetAccount.

Furner also said Walmart is seeing encouraging adoption of its artificial intelligence agent, named Sparky, especially ahead of the holiday season. He said it's led to customer basket increases of 40% on average.

"It just is a great way of just completing the entire basket and solving entire the solution for people," he said.

Sam's Club reported net sales of $25.9 billion, marking a 10% increase from the year-ago period. It said its sales were driven by strength in the grocery category and general merchandise.

In October, Walmart announced it was launching a joint membership option with Walmart+ and Sam's Club as it leans further into convenience. Both stores will still offer stand-alone memberships, as well.

Furner told CNBC the new membership has brought roughly 2 million new people into Sam's Club since its launch in late October. Though the company reimbursed some members for the new membership rate, Rainey added that it's already seeing positive returns on the joint model and that consumers are "spending appreciably more."

The company on Thursday said its global membership fee revenue grew 17% in the third quarter.

The company is typically well insulated from macroeconomic pressures because of its value reputation. Last quarter, the company said it was lowering prices of products across multiple categories, including beef.

Rainey said Thursday that he's "optimistic" about consumer spending heading into the holiday season and that the fourth quarter is already "off to a pretty good start."

The big-box retailer has posted solid growth over the past few quarters as it sees customers gravitate to reduced prices, improvements in delivery and gains in its advertising business. It's also leaned into attracting higher-income consumers with its Walmart+ membership.

Shares of Walmart have fluctuated this year but are up roughly 13% year to date.

#115. Gap stock soars as retailer says Old Navy turnaround shows signs of improvement

Gap Inc. on Thursday announced Ashley Kechter will be the next president and CEO of its Athleta brand, effective Dec. 14.

The retailer also said Old Navy's turnaround is showing signs of progress, sending shares up more than 12% in trading Friday.

Kechter comes to Gap from activewear retailer Vuori, where she was global president and oversaw the brand's global business strategy, marketing and financials. She was previously global brand president of Fabletics and began her career at Gap before that.

"I'm excited to return to Gap Inc. and partner with [Gap CEO Richard Dickson] and the team to strengthen our connection with customers and shape the brand's next chapter," Kechter said in a statement.

The announcement comes after Lululemon poached former Athleta CEO Maggie Gauger to be its chief product officer last month.

"Clearly, we're disappointed with Athleta's performance, and we recognize there's a lot of work ahead," Dickson told CNBC. "We had been in rebuild mode, but now we're really in turnaround mode."

Dickson said the company is focused on getting back to its fundamentals: fabrications, feature, form, function and fit. Dickson added that Kechter will be conducting a "comprehensive review" of the brand to reposition Athleta for future success.

While the execution of its strategy has thus far "fallen short," Dickson said this new approach will allow Athleta to fully realize its potential.

Gap also reported mixed fiscal third-quarter earnings on Thursday and posted flat overall comparable sales. Wall Street had been expecting a comparable sales increase of 1.3%, according to StreetAccount.

Net sales grew 2%, though demand softened in October and early November due to warmer weather, the company said. Store sales increased 1%, while online sales increased 3%.

Gap also said it's recorded a $420 million adjustment to cost of goods sold and a $21 million adjustment to interest income from tariff refunds so far this year.

The company lowered its full-year net sales outlook to be "roughly flat," down from previous guidance of up between 1% and 1.5%. Gap is also now expecting adjusted earnings per share of between $2.29 and $2.34, down from an earlier expectation of between $2.35 and $2.45 per share.

Dickson said in an interview that much of the lowered guidance was due to warmer weather in the first two weeks of the quarter, but he said Gap is expecting "meaningful acceleration" in shopping ahead of the peak holiday season.

"We're monitoring the trends, and we're going to remain agile as we respond to the changing business trends," Dickson said.

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter, Gap reported net income of $311 million, or 86 cents per share, compared with $236 million, or 62 cents per share, the year prior. Sales rose slightly from $3.94 billion the year prior.

At Athleta, net sales were down 14% compared with last year, coming in at $220 million. Comparable sales for the banner sank 15%, which the company attributed to "limited traction" from efforts to modernize its merchandise and marketing. `

The company's namesake brand saw 9% comparable sales growth for the quarter, with net sales of $1.1 billion, marking an 11% increase compared with last year. Dickson said it was the brand's 12th consecutive quarter of positive comparable sales.

"Gap is really firing on all cylinders, and the consistency of the brand is really giving us confidence in the brand's ability to continue its momentum," Dickson said.

The Old Navy banner saw flat comparable sales for the quarter and net sales of $2.3 billion, up 1% compared with last year. It's the first earnings report since the brand, which has been experiencing sluggish sales, named Michael Francis as its new CEO last quarter. Francis began his role at Old Navy on Nov. 2.

Banana Republic saw its net sales sink 3% compared with last year, which the company attributed to delayed demand for cold-weather categories and a women's fall assortment that fell flat. Wall Street had been expecting growth of 1.5%, according to StreetAccount.

Dickson said the brand has since introduced new products and seen demand "rebound" as cooler weather has set in across the country.

"We've been encouraged by the early customer response, and we should see sequential improvement in the fourth quarter," he said. "There's some work to do here, but with peak selling still ahead, I'm confident that the team is focused on the right areas and [will] ultimately position Banana well for growth."
