#114. Fish and eggs al desko? The dos and don'ts of lunch at work

There are two types of office workers: those who eat lunch al-desko, and those who have to endure the smell of everyone else's.

Few things are more likely to test workplace relationships than the person sitting next to you proudly unveiling a container of reheated fish.

From tuna sandwiches and boiled eggs to crumbly and sticky treats that leave a mess on the keyboard, what is considered OK to eat in the office can be surprisingly divisive.

"We all know not to bring in a microwave fish pie, that is the law," says civil servant Sarah.

She admits she has, in fact, committed the offence herself in a previous workplace and jokes "that's the reason I left".

Casey, a content creator for an energy company, says he used to eat tuna at his desk but has since "reformed".

He says you have to "suss out the smelliness" and then decide if it's acceptable to eat at the desk.

Casey and Manny often eat at their desk and their favourite pretzel snack is causing some problems given how sticky and crumbly it is

But fish isn't the only food causing office friction and Casey says his regular snack of cinnamon pretzels has been causing "quite a bit of havoc".

"The sugar's going everywhere and it's getting in my laptop keys," he admits. No-one in the office has commented on it yet but he says he's hiding the crumbs by "shuffling them off the desk".

Boiled eggs also split opinion. Laura, who works in finance, says she's been teased by colleagues for eating them.

"In my defence, it was high protein, it was cost-effective and healthy â I swear by a good boiled egg."

She thinks that no food is off limits at the desk. "Coming from an Asian background where you'd eat fish for breakfast I am very open to it being eaten."

Ashlea Fisher says it's important all of her employees are mindful about the food they are eating at their desk

Ashlea Fisher, who runs her own recruitment company, has tackled the issue of smelly food in the office head on when she asked a member of staff to stop bringing in fish after "the smell was so bad that a pregnant colleague was feeling physically sick".

"I'm a big believer in being able to have an honest conversation with your team," she says, and suggests that other people in her position should approach a conversation lightly and be conscious of not embarrassing colleagues.

"Nobody should feel judged for what they eat, and we should respect different cultures and dietary requirements, but being mindful of the people around you is simply good workplace etiquette."

While what we eat in the office at lunch can cause tension, there's also the question of whether we're even taking the time to enjoy our lunch in the first place.

In a world of back-to-back meetings and overflowing inboxes, it's easy to skip our break and shovel down a sandwich with one hand while replying to emails with the other.

A 2025 report by Just Eat found that the youngest workers, Gen Z, were the most likely to take a full break, with 56% doing so.

However, the next generation up, millennials were more likely than any other to eat lunch at their desk.

"While it can be convenient to eat at your desk, it's not best practice to remain glued to the screen all day," says Alan Price, an employment law and HR expert, and CEO of BrightHR.

"Taking a break isn't just an employment right, there are proven benefits for both physical and mental health."

Finance worker Laura takes a lunch and dinner break when she's working long hours

Laura, whose working day can occasionally finish at 2am, says stepping away from her desk is a non-negotiable and she makes a point of taking an hour for lunch, describing it as "essential for maintaining my sanity".

"I take my lunch break as much as possible, for the sake of my eyes and mental wellbeing," she says.

But others are less precious about it. Content creator Manny says he has lunch at his desk "most of the time".

His work is task-based and his company operates a policy of "do the work when you want to do it".

"Usually you just take 20 minutes to eat something, but on another day you might take an hour. You just kind of feel it out," he says.

#112. Tinned food and long-life milk boost retail sales after 'Be Ready' campaign launch

Retail sales rose in October, driven by people buying long-life food such as tinned goods and UHT milk, according to official figures.

Sales volumes were 0.7% higher as people responded to the government's emergency preparedness "Be Ready" campaign, the Office for National Statistics (ONS) said.

The campaign encouraged people to stock up in case of disruption to essential services by severe weather or cyberattacks.

Separate research released last week suggested that while households were stockpiling essentials, they were not panic buying.

The government campaign encouraged people to keep three days' worth of food and water in their homes.

Wet wipes, a torch and cash were also recommended.

Data from retail research firm Worldpanel by Numerator indicated last week that people were stocking up on items including tinned meat, bottled water and hand sanitiser.

The latest official sales figures, external from the ONS add to the evidence that the public has begun stockpiling.

"Supermarkets have told us that sales of long-life food items such as tinned food and UHT milk were boosted by the government's 'Be Ready' campaign," ONS chief economist Grant Fitzner said.

"Meanwhile fuel sales have been recovering from earlier low levels, despite pump prices rising again."

The rise in retail sales last month beat some economists' expectations - they had predicted a slight fall - and the increase came after a 0.6% decline in September.

As well as sales being boosted by people buying essentials, visits to art galleries and clothing purchases were also higher in October.

The boost in sales comes at the beginning of the festive period, which is normally the busiest time of the year for retailers.

Jacqui Baker, partner and head of retail at auditing firm RSM UK, said that "retailers will want to see out the year with some Christmas cheer before consumers batten down the hatches in the New Year as energy costs skyrocket and inflation ramps up further".

Typical energy bills are forecast to go up by Â£443 per year from January, pushing up the pace of price rises.

Sales of tinned food and bottled water jump after 'Be Ready' campaign launch

Why should I stockpile and what will I need?

Separate economic data released on Friday showed the government borrowed slightly less than economists had expected in October.

Borrowing - the difference between public sector spending and income - was Â£17bn last month, the ONS said., external

Although that was Â£0.8bn higher than October last year, it was below analysts' forecasts of about Â£18bn.

Interest repayments were Â£11.2bn - the highest October debt interest figure on record - and Â£2.8bn more than a year ago.

These repayments have been pushed up by higher inflation, as payments on index-linked government bonds are linked to the inflation rate.

Inflation rose to 3.6% in October, mainly because of higher petrol and diesel prices. The rate has been rising in recent months as the Iran war has driven up the cost of energy and fuel.

#110. Jensen Huang's — and Nvidia's — legal exposure balloons

The still-unraveling scandal of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's extramarital affairs and the tech giant's potential role in its advanced semiconductor chips ending up in China present a legal minefield for Huang and Nvidia.

Federal and state prosecutors and Nvidia employees may target Huang and the company in court, where some shareholders have already taken action.

That's on top of a rapidly growing pile of probes by Republicans who control Congress now and Democrats who will have majorities in both the House and Senate come January.

"It's not going to get any better for Jensen Huang; it's going to get far worse," said Salvatore Graziano, a New York securities litigation attorney at Bernstein, Litowitz, Berger & Grossmann. His firm is looking at shareholder lawsuit opportunities against Nvidia and Huang.

[Huang] "faces the Department of Justice, the Securities and Exchange Commission and private litigation," Graziano said. "The criminal exposure is very serious. The SEC could bar him from being an officer. And the potential liability [from shareholder] lawsuits can be very large."

Huang's and Nvidia's legal exposure stems from three issues that led up to his shock resignation Nov. 12.

There's his alleged affair with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang, who worked for Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company under investigation for diverting advanced Nvidia semiconductor chips to China in violation of U.S. export rules. That, in turn, has raised questions about whether Huang and Nvidia were aware of any diversions.

There are Huang's two other alleged affairs with Nvidia employees.

And there is the sharp drop in the company's stock price in the wake of the scandal. Nvidia's share price has fallen 19% since Nov. 8, when Huang sent a company-wide apology acknowledging extramarital affairs.

Huang last week retained the law firm Paul, Weiss and its attorneys Melinda Haag and Walter Brown to represent him. Haag is a former U.S. attorney in San Francisco, and Brown is also a former federal prosecutor.

Huang's spokesman at Trident, a crisis communications firm, declined to comment when asked if Haag and Brown are representing him in any potential criminal case and other questions about his legal exposure.

Nvidia did not respond to a request for comment. Last week, Nvidia retained the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct an independent investigation into Huang allegedly being targeted by a spy, as U.S. intelligence reportedly has concluded.

The civil liability Huang and Nvidia could face is underscored by a hearing Graziano, the securities attorney, attended Tuesday in U.S. District Court in San Francisco. A judge at the hearing approved a $175 million settlement from Seagate Technology Holdings to resolve a shareholder lawsuit alleging it concealed sales of hard-disk drives to Huawei in China in violation of export bans.

The Seagate settlement came three years after the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security — which is investigating Megaspeed's business with Nvidia — imposed a $300 million penalty on Seagate for its sales to Huawei.

Graziano said his law firm is "actively looking at securities law liability for Nvidia, Jensen Huang and other senior executives in the company given all the recent developments regarding their [potential] knowledge and facilitation of illegal chip sales to Chinese entities."

There is already a pending class-action shareholder lawsuit in San Francisco federal court against Nvidia and Huang by the Rosen Law Firm, for allegedly misleading and false statements related to Megaspeed and Alice Huang.

"Several hundreds of investors have contacted us and expressed an interest to join the case — investors of all walks of life, from mom and pops to investors with very large losses," Rosen attorney Phil Kim told CNBC in an email.

Katie Sweeten, a former federal prosecutor in the DOJ's National Security Division, said the question of "who knew what and when, and what was [Huang's] role" is potentially the biggest risk for Nvidia and him.

"Now DOJ can leverage their criminal authorities," Sweeten, a partner at Scale LLP, told CNBC's Kelly Evans on "The Exchange."

"They can bring grand jury subpoenas, search warrants for emails, bank accounts, personal devices, computers, things like that," Sweeten said.

While it is "not illegal to, you know, sleep with a spy ... it is illegal to pass certain types of information to a counterintelligence officer," she said.

Sweeten also pointed to potential export control violations, which "could be very serious."

She noted the DOJ can indict a company as well as its officials if there is evidence of criminality. Export control violations carry a maximum possible criminal sentence of 20 years in prison and "really large fines," she said.

"You can't send the company to prison, but they can face fines, civil penalties, debarment — where you can't export items down the line," she said.

One example of that came in July 2025, when the DOJ obtained a guilty plea and an agreement to pay more than $140 million from Cadence Design Systems for violating the Export Control Reform Act by unlawfully exporting semiconductor design tools to China's National University of Defense Technology, a school led by China's Central Military Commission.

Another potential legal risk for Huang comes from his planned appearances before Congress.

Huang is scheduled to give a closed-door interview Dec. 1 to three House committees. Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., wants Huang's testimony before that panel to be public, and told CNBC Tuesday he hopes to have that happen "before Christmas."

Sweeten said Huang "has to be very careful" about what he says there because of the risk of being criminally charged with lying to Congress, and of saying something "that absolutely can become part of a criminal or civil investigation."

"I expect Huang to take the Fifth on most questions" from Congress, Jonathan Kanter, a former assistant attorney general in the DOJ's Antitrust Division, told CNBC, meaning Huang will likely use his constitutional right against giving testimony that could be used against him in a criminal case.

"With a Justice Department criminal investigation into Megaspeed already underway and more investigations possible, anything he tells Congress could be used against him," Kanter said. "His lawyers are likely to advise him against taking that risk."

Ben Heineman Jr., a former senior vice president and general counsel of General Electric, and a senior fellow at Harvard University, told CNBC that Nvidia's board will be scrutinized over its actions before it pushed Huang to resign.

Heineman cited the board's apparent lack of succession planning and what looks like weak oversight of Huang, including relating to personal conduct such as the two alleged affairs with Nvidia employees and with an executive of an Nvidia customer.

"When there is a sort of reasonable suspicion, they need to investigate it, and they don't appear to have done that," Heineman said.

Nvidia and Huang could face investigation from state attorneys general, including in California, where the company is headquartered.

On Tuesday, California Attorney General Rob Bonta told Bloomberg Television that the situation at Nvidia with Huang is "something that we're interested in." Bonta's office later told CNBC, "We are monitoring the situation."

Huang's alleged affairs with Nvidia employees also open the possibility of workplace litigation.

Ari Wilkenfeld, a Washington-area employment lawyer, said a consensual relationship between a chief executive and a subordinate "is not in and of itself illegal."

"The liability never comes from the existence of the relationship alone. It comes from whether it was genuinely welcome, what job benefits flowed from it, and what it did to everyone else in the building," Wilkenfeld said. "California law recognizes that so-called sexual favoritism can create a hostile environment for an entire workforce.

"The affair is Huang's problem," Wilkenfeld said. "What, if anything, Nvidia did once it knew is the company's problem."

#108. Faisal Islam: This cost-cutting UK-EU deal doesn't mean lower prices will follow

The prime minister made a lot of the UK's new deal with the EU cutting costs for business and consumers during tough times.

In another world, Andy Burnham would have loved for the nation's supermarkets to clearly announce that they could cut food prices as a result of this latest agreement on food exports.

And while grocers have very much welcomed Friday's announcement, they have notably not committed to price cuts as a direct result of this move.

The managing director of M&S, Alex Freudmann, called the deal a "major step forward", saying it would remove bureaucracy. Tesco chief executive Ken Murphy added it was "good news for British farmers and producers".

Freudmann highlighted some of hoops that were hurting UK-EU trade, using the example that "getting our spaghetti Bolognese from Lincolnshire onto a shelf in Dublin takes hundreds of extra miles, mountains of paperwork, a vet's signature and even the Latin name of a cow".

Such measures will no longer need to be taken once this deal kicks in. Good news for business exports then, but will that lead to lower shopping bills here in the UK?

This deal will matter for food exporters - it may not have a clear visible impact at the checkout.

Privately, industry insiders say there are too many moving parts in the world economy, especially given the energy price shock resulting from war in the Middle East.

It is more a hope than will at this stage. The industry and government hope that the reduction in costs for food producers and sellers will be passed on to customers.

This is not the first time Labour has sought to reduce the weekly shop costs. Sir Keir Starmer's administration floated the idea that supermarkets might even offer some form of branded discounts, a suggestion that was met with collective bemusement by those in the industry.

However, there will be less paperwork, checks and red tape on food imports too, but they were already lightly enforced.

No longer will hundreds of pages of documents be required. No longer will perishable goods lose valuable hours or days at border checks. No longer will the incorrect colour of ink or Latin name for a meat disallow a shipment of British exports.

Some, though not all of the 24% hit since 2019 to the real value of agrifood exports, could be regained by a bonfire of this red tape.

In particular, smaller exporters of food will be able to return to the large EU market.

But the extra benefit here is unclear, and there could be extra checks and costs required instead on non-EU food imports.

The government's own assessment is that the economy will gain Â£5bn through this, or 0.14% by 2040.

Critics point out that this benefit does not include the quid pro quo - having to align with EU laws. Farmers have pointed to compliance with pesticides and fungicide regulation.

The classic example of the benefits of post-Brexit autonomy has been the development of precision-bred foodstuffs, such as Vitamin D-enhanced tomatoes.

The producer of this technology has, however, backed the deal after the government negotiated an "exception period" for ongoing UK sale.

Professor Cristobal Uauy of the John Innes Centre said that "aligning with the EU market will bring our science to a greater number of people".

Bringing all the agreements on food, emissions and the youth experience together, the economy is forecast to get an overall boost of 0.5%, or Â£17bn a year.

A modest boost, but high by the standards of past post-Brexit trade deals, for example, with Australia, India or Japan and the Gulf and the trans-pacific deal.

This UK-EU pact actually matches all of those agreements combined for expected growth measured by the Treasury's own assessments.

The biggest single boost to the overall economy would come from more European young workers. However, the economy benefit of this part of the deal per person reduces to zero, according to the government's own calculations.

Burham also said he hoped a planned electricity deal with the EU would cut bills, but that has not been done yet, and requires a contribution into the bloc's budget for poorer regions.

The electricity deal could help lower bills, but that contains a clear trade-off.

There are still some details to be revealed in the full legal texts of these documents.

The bigger point is whether this logic of gaining free-flowing access for food, in exchange for giving up regulatory freedom, will now apply elsewhere.

Is food now a blueprint for future negotiations? It had been the Starmer-Rachel Reeves strategy to spread this model to other sectors such as cars, and chemicals.

The big takeaway from today was that both the PM and his counterparts publicly acknowledged the end of the incremental piecemeal approach to aligning with the single market.

The question is whether the PM thinks what goes for food applies across the whole economy. Most economists predict that could lead to much bigger economic gains, but it will also be a much bigger constitutional battle.

#107. A24 is having a great year at the box office. That could give HBO Max a streaming edge

Independent movie studio A24 has had a banner year at the box office, and HBO Max is set up to benefit from its success.

A24 is behind some of this year's buzziest films, including horror hit "Backrooms," dark comedy "The Drama" starring Zendaya and Robert Pattinson, and the Elizabeth Holmes documentary "You Can See Everything," co-directed by Nathan Fielder.

The studio has generated more than $526 million at the domestic box office this year as of Nov. 15, according to data from Rentrak. That's already roughly $290 million, or more than 50%, above its haul for all of 2025.

"They have an identity all their own, and just the mere mention of A24, the recognition that a movie is from A24, gives it instant credibility and provides a must-see factor, no matter what the subject matter," Paul Dergarabedian, head of marketplace trends at Rentrak, told CNBC.

Skydance co-CEO and Chairman David Ellison cited movie releases from A24 as part of the company's "unmatched portfolio" during its most recent earnings call.

A24 films released this year broke records at the box office, with "Backrooms" becoming the studio's biggest domestic opening to date and "You Can See Everything" notching the biggest documentary opening since "Fahrenheit 9/11" in 2004.

That success has already begun to translate to HBO Max's platform.

When "Backrooms" began streaming on HBO Max, it was the most streamed title on the platform for two weeks, according to Nielsen data. The film was also the fourth overall most streamed title the week it premiered, racking up 226 million minutes of viewing time.

After more than a year of competing bids and regulatory hurdles, Skydance now oversees two of the world's largest streaming platforms: HBO Max and Paramount+.

The company plans to eventually merge the streaming services into a single platform. It will take the first step in that effort by offering a value bundle in early 2027.

The combination of the platforms "will drive stronger engagement and higher lifetime value," co-CEO Ynon Kreiz said on the company's earnings call earlier this month.

Paramount Skydance's direct-to-consumer segment, which includes Paramount+ and its free, ad-supported channel, Pluto TV, generated more than $2 billion in revenue during the third quarter, representing more than 35% of total revenue for the period. That segment revenue marked an 11% year-over-year increase, with revenue from Paramount+ up 21%.

The earnings do not reflect the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, since that deal closed after the close of the quarter.

Revenue growth in streaming will be important as it faces the daunting task of managing nearly $80 billion of debt.

The company has said it expects to realize $6 billion in cost savings over the next three years and has put a particular focus on consolidating its operations and streamlining its marketing spend.

#105. Anthropic shares fall after blockbuster opening as wider tech sell-off continues

Watch: How did Anthropic's stock market debut unfold?

Anthropic's shares have dropped back towards their initial price after a soaring first day on the stock market, as a wider tech sell-off deepens.

The fall in tech stocks continued to weigh on markets, with the S&P 500 down more than 2% and the Dow Jones Industrial Average down 0.7% by lunchtime.

Both US indexes touched correction territory, a point where they are trading at 10% or more down from a recent high.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index also fell more than 3.4% by the middle of trading on Friday.

The speed at which AI investment has been growing - with more than $1 trillion spent in the last year alone by a small handful of major tech companies - has led to ongoing concerns of a financial bubble. Questions are being raised over whether those investments will pay off, and when.

Despite a positive first day of trading, Anthropic's shares fell on Friday, dropping more than 12% to about $85 by lunchtime, close to its initial public offering (IPO) price of $83.

The artificial intelligence (AI) company's introduction to the Nasdaq stock exchange on Thursday was a crucial test for the industry and was largely seen as a success, with trading starting at $111.50.

The maker of the Claude chatbot raised a record $81bn in its initial public offering (IPO), but its share price moved significantly in the first day of trading.

The shares peaked at $115 on Thursday, before easing to end the day up 17% at $97.17.

Analysts said volatility was to be expected in the first days or even weeks of Anthropic's trading, as the company was the first frontier AI firm to go public so had no direct peers.

Some have also noted the similarities with Elon Musk's SpaceX, which at the time of its debut in June was the largest IPO on record. Shares in the rocket and AI company experienced large swings in its first few weeks of trading.

Most tech companies see their shares fall after hitting the stock market, often after reporting quarterly results for the first time.

But many, including Meta, Amazon and Google, have gone on to become the largest and most profitable corporations in the world.

Ed Moya, a strategist at market intelligence firm AlphaSense, said Anthropic's blockbuster IPO did not appear to have restored confidence in the broader AI market.

While AI demand was still very strong, Moya told the BBC that investors were trying to answer the question of how much were they willing to pay for that growth.

"As we've gone through the past past few weeks, it seems that there's been more risks that have surfaced," he said.

"Right now you're starting to see Wall Street is trying to shift from rewarding growth at any price, to demanding stronger cash flow and a clearer path to profitability."

Shares in some of the largest US tech companies also fell on Friday.

Nvidia and SpaceX were both down more than 6%, while Tesla shares fell more than 3% and Meta was down 3.4%.

Apple, Amazon and Microsoft experienced smaller falls, while Alphabet was up by 1.2%.

Europe's main stock markets - which contain fewer tech stocks - also took a hit.

Germany's Dax index closed down 2.6%, France's CAC 40 was down 1.9% and the UK's FTSE 100 ended trading 1% lower.

Earlier on Friday, tech-heavy Asian markets saw steep falls.

South Korea's Kospi and Japan's Nikkei 225 indexes closed down about 5%, with shares in memory chip makers Sk Hynix, Kioxia and Tokyo Electron, and other tech firms such as Samsung Electronics, seeing losses.

Taiwan's stock market dropped about 6% on Friday, taking the weekly fall to more than 17% and what was reported to be its biggest weekly fall since 1990.

Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

#104. Forget Anthropic: In Europe, the IPO hype is about a blue lagoon

While investors are fixated on Anthropic's blockbuster stock market debut, a very different IPO is making waves in Europe: Iceland's Blue Lagoon, the operator of one of the world's most famous geothermal spas.

Blue Lagoon Corp. raised roughly 14.3 billion Icelandic kronur ($110 million) through the offering, valuing the Icelandic geothermal spa operator at about $787 million, according to the company's investor presentation.

The Blue Lagoon traces its origins to 1992, when Blue Lagoon Ltd. was founded to study the water in the Blue Lagoon, an open-air pool of warm, milky-blue water set in a black lava field in southwestern Iceland. It grew into a business spanning a spa, hotels and skincare made with ingredients from the lagoon.

Shares will trade under the ticker "BLUE" on one of Europe's smallest stock exchanges, Nasdaq Iceland Main Market, part of the Nasdaq Nordic group, which also operates exchanges in Sweden, Denmark and Finland.

"Wellness is the new luxury, and we know that the wellness economy is growing faster than the economy as a whole, so we have tailwind from that," CEO Sigríður Margrét Oddsdóttir told CNBC on Thursday.

"Now is the right time for us to list," she added. "We are a company that thinks long-term, we have a clear vision for the next decade, and being publicly listed gives investors the opportunity to participate in our journey."

The company's operations are centered on Iceland's Reykjanes Peninsula, where, as well as the Blue Lagoon geothermal spa, it runs the Silica Hotel, the Retreat luxury hotel and spa, and the Michelin-starred Moss restaurant.

It also operates the Kerlingarfjöll Highland Base and Hrauneyjar properties in Iceland's highlands, and sells Blue Lagoon-branded skincare products internationally.

The 34-year-old company has averaged 11% annual revenue growth over the past decade, and next year is expected to be a record, with revenue growing by more than half, Oddsdóttir said.

The company plans to use the raised capital to fund expanding its existing properties, acquiring businesses, and developing new destinations.

In total, nearly 4,000 subscriptions were received in the initial public offering, worth about 15.3 billion kronur, about 7% more than the total size of the offering, the company said, citing strong demand from smaller investors, including retail investors.

Iceland's relatively small stock market had just 27 companies on its main exchange at the end of 2025, according to Nasdaq. The market is dominated by banks, seafood companies and other domestically focused firms.

The global wellness industry reached $7.5 trillion in 2025, up 8.9% from the previous year, according to the Global Wellness Institute. Spending on thermal and mineral springs grew 10.4% over the same period, with the institute forecasting annual growth of nearly 10% for the sector through 2030.

Tourism accounted for nearly 9% of Iceland's GDP in 2025, and is growing, according to Statistics Iceland. The country welcomed around 2.3 million international visitors that year, with the U.S. as its largest source market.

#102. Warsh wants Fed officials to talk less. Instead, they’re talking more

In his first six months as Federal Reserve chairman, Kevin Warsh has diligently avoided signaling where he sees monetary policy and interest rates headed. His colleagues have been only too happy to fill the void.

Fed officials have been rushing to the microphones lately, voicing sometimes conflicting opinions on how the central bank should be handling an inflation bug that the economy can't seem to shake.

Most agree that rates should be raised — and soon. Some are willing to hike at a measured pace as conditions play out, while others, such as Governor Christopher Waller and St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem, entertain the thought of more aggressive hikes in a shorter period of time.

Largely on the sidelines in the debate is Warsh, who has expressed disdain for that sort of forward guidance and argued that it handcuffs policymakers long before they actually have to make a decision. But that hasn't stopped most of the other 18 officials who sit in on Federal Open Market Committee meetings.

In fact, according to a Bank of America analysis, there has been more so-called Fedspeak in the past two weeks than in any similar period since the start of 2025.

"Warsh's refusal to guide markets might be encouraging the rest of the FOMC to speak more, not less," Bank of America economist Aditya Bhave said in a note Thursday.

"There is always a range of views across the 19 FOMC participants. Typically, the Chair would weigh in, indicating to markets whether the hawks or doves are in ascendence," he added. "Absent this leadership, however, both sides might feel incentivized to speak more often and more forcefully."

The increasingly vocal debate comes at a critical time for the Fed and has myriad policy implications.

Bhave noted "a deepening divide" on the FOMC. The Fed's decision not to increase its benchmark rate at its October meeting "increasingly looks like a mistake," and as a result "the hawks have been very vocal of late," he wrote. Leading the charge is Waller, who has raised the possibility of hiking by half a percentage point when the committee next meets in December.

Minutes from the October meeting hint at the tension, particularly when it comes to Warsh's refusal to provide guidance.

The summary related that some officials who were looking for a hike in October "could have supported no change in the federal funds rate provided that it was clearly understood by market participants that further tightening was likely" — a statement that points toward the forward guidance that Warsh has insisted the Fed avoid.

The minutes "revealed that some officials favoring a hike would have accepted a hold if the Fed had clearly communicated further tightening," market veteran Ed Yardeni wrote Thursday. "Warsh's communication philosophy may thus be limiting the Fed's flexibility precisely when it needs it most."

Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack, who voted against the October pause in favor of a quarter-point hike, tiptoed around the forward guidance issue during a recent public appearance.

Prefacing some observations she made about where she sees policy headed, Hammack noted that, "Those are not promises. Those are not commitments. They're not forward guidance, which is a hot-button topic. But what they are are a conditional set of expectations of where I think the economy can go, based on what I'm seeing."

Hammack's comments illustrate a needle that Fed officials have been trying to thread: continuing to be transparent about their thinking without appearing locked into a future policy that changing economic conditions could render obsolete.

Speaking Thursday, Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee framed the argument around credibility.

"I am sympathetic to the view that [Warsh] has expressed that let's not be so, in in our language, forward guidance-oriented, saying here is what I think rates are going to be at the next meeting," Goolsbee said, calling such pronouncements "falsely precise."

However, he quickly separated forward guidance from transparency.

"I think central banks should be careful about doing anything that undermines credibility," Goolsbee said. "That said, I also think that it's pretty important [that] each person who sits around that table explain how they see the world. What do you see in business conditions? You don't want to surprise everybody."

Taking a slightly different tack, Philadelphia Fed President Anna Paulson, in a speech Thursday on policy communication, insisted that markets can figure out where policymakers are going without specific guidance so long as they understand how economic developments will influence decisions.

Paulson also defended the variety of voices coming from the Fed.

"But volume is not the same as clarity," she said. "The measure is not how much we say. It is whether we are understood." The mixture of voices "guards against any single communication becoming the only one that matters."

Financial markets have taken the plethora of Fed communications and interpreted it in a fairly hawkish way.

Traders are implying a 100% chance of at least a quarter percentage point hike in December, with a very small probability of a half-point move, according to the CME Group's FedWatch. From there, odds are high for a follow-up increase in January, then perhaps one more before the end of 2027.

While current inflation indicators are running above 3%, compared to the Fed's 2% target, market measures of longer-term inflation have declined.

Five-year forward expectations, as indicated through the Treasury market, are at their lowest since April. That suggests investors could be less concerned about inflation over the longer term, even as policymakers warn that current price pressures remain elevated.

Still, a sclerotic communications strategy from the Fed amid volatile economic and geopolitical currents could prove dangerous ahead.

"The result of this back-and-forth is undue rates volatility in already turbulent times," BofA's Bhave said.

For his part, Warsh has been advocating for a "family fight" atmosphere inside the Fed, arguing that competing viewpoints deserve a full airing as the committee works towards decisions.

"There are benefits to shaping the market's future expectations. Perhaps this is part of the 'family fight' that Warsh keeps referencing in his pressers," said Tom Graff, chief investment officer at advisory firm Facet. "That family fight seems to be happening out in public."

#101. Rejecting cheap fabrics, higher prices: Why shoppers are asking for better clothes

Clothing isn't made like it used to be, and more shoppers are taking notice.

The makeup of apparel imported into the U.S. has shifted in recent years. In 2010, about 60% of clothing was predominantly made from cotton. Now, that total is around 45%, while predominantly synthetic fabrics such as polyester and nylon comprise the majority of imports.

Apparel prices generally declined over the two decades leading up to the pandemic, according to the consumer price index. Since 2020, they have soared to record highs.

It's starting to change habits for consumers who are paying more for food, gas, rent and other staples. As shoppers shell out more for clothes, they're increasingly making sure the garments are worth the cost.

Consulting firm AlixPartners' 2026 Consumer Sentiment Index, which surveyed more than 9,000 shoppers, found that consumers are scrutinizing labels more closely and rejecting cheap materials sold at a premium.

"If it's not a natural material, a retailer brand who passes it off as it is, they're really becoming very low on consumers' minds," said Sonia Lapinsky, head of fashion at AlixPartners.

Fast fashion brands like Forever 21 and H&M exploded in popularity during the early 2000s and 2010s, bringing trendy clothing to shelves quickly at low prices. Then came ultra-fast fashion, with brands like Shein and Fashion Nova pumping out new garments at even quicker speeds and lower price points — made possible in part through the use of inexpensive, synthetic fabrics.

"We got to a point where the pendulum just swung too far in that direction. And we've actually begun to see a reversal," said U.S. Bancorp BTIG consumer analyst Janine Stichter. "Quality matters a lot more ... over the last three or four years than it has in the previous 10 or 20."

But quality, which shoppers often tie directly to material, can come at a higher cost.

The average unit price of apparel imported to the U.S. between January and August was $2.64 per square meter for garments that were predominantly synthetic fiber, like polyester and nylon, compared with $3.67 for items that were largely cotton, according to data from the U.S. Commerce Department's Office of Textiles and Apparel.

When comparing those 2026 figures to 2010, synthetic fiber apparel import costs have climbed around 11%, while cotton apparel costs have risen 21%.

"If you're appealing to a more value-sensitive customer, you either reduce the quality, or you negotiate with suppliers to try to share some of the cost or you maybe absorb the cost … and perhaps hope to crank a higher volume of product to make up for it," said Bernstein Research senior retail analyst Aneesha Sherman.

Though polyester is cheaper, experts point out that's not necessarily a bad thing. It can help clothing hold its shape and wick away sweat.

Sherman believes the athleisure boom is a primary factor in the shift toward polyester. She said that from 2015 to 2025, sports apparel was growing at a 4.9% rate in the U.S., versus 2.6% for all apparel and footwear, according to Euromonitor data.

"There probably is some quality trade down as well happening there," Sherman said. "But I think both these trends are conflated."

Nonetheless, backlash to synthetic materials is growing. Gen Z has even repurposed the word "polyester" as an insult to mean generally low-quality or fake.

Concerns have also grown about the environment and human health surrounding petroleum-based clothing, as more research indicates microplastics can leach from polyester clothing when washed.

It's led consumers to reexamine what materials are in their clothing.

Apparel prices have risen coming out of the pandemic, as demand outpaced supply and allowed companies to raise prices, said BTIG's Stichter. Tariffs and the Iran war followed, raising costs for businesses — and prices for consumers.

Petroleum is the basis of many synthetic fibers, and oil prices have skyrocketed since the start of the Iran war.

The same factors that helped drive up prices of clothing — Covid, tariffs and war — also affected clothing quality.

"Companies had to move around their supply chains in the post-Covid period," said Stichter. "They had to find ways to take costs out, and sometimes that meant using less expensive fabrics."

Those factors compounded a more-than-decade-long shift away from manufacturing in China.

Sheng Lu, a fashion and apparel studies professor at the University of Delaware, whose research focuses on the economics of the global textile industry, said China's established history with clothing production means it has higher-skilled workers, state-of-the-art-equipment and better material management compared with emerging manufacturing destinations such as Bangladesh, Sri Lanka and Cambodia.

"They mostly serve the value market and the mass market. And I think the reason why they're not ready to make higher-quality luxury and premium products really reflects the kind of skill sets you can expect when you source from these countries," said Lu.

AlixPartners' Lapinsky said these countries that manufacture at a lower cost sometimes have to still ship the raw materials from China, which can extend the timeline to make clothing. That means taking earlier bets on trends and then discounting if products don't sell well.

"If you have to mark down that product because the consumer doesn't want it as much, your net margin might actually be worse than if you were to just invest in quality in the first place," said BTIG's Stichter.

And as material costs rise for synthetic fibers, Stichter also said she believes it could further influence companies to use more natural fabrics moving forward.

Companies prioritizing quality are winning in this environment.

Ralph Lauren's stock is up around 85% over the past two years. Around 90% of its apparel is primarily made of cotton, leather, wool, viscose, cashmere or silk, according to its 2026 sustainability report. In its fiscal 2026, the company said it sold more products at full price and reduced markdowns.

Meanwhile, direct-to-consumer clothing brand Quince, which centers its brand identity around natural materials, reached a $10 billion valuation this year.

The brand jumped 16 spots to No. 3 for best resale value in online consignment retailer ThredUp's 2025 report. Organic cotton retailer Pact jumped 20 spots on that list to No. 8, illustrating the potential role materials are playing in brand recognition.

Alternatively, the 2026 AlixPartners report, which ranks sentiment for brands in different retail categories, found shoppers are viewing some fast fashion brands less favorably. Both Shein and Zara dropped three spots — to fifth and seventh place, respectively — in terms of sentiment in the young adult specialty category.

Shein passed higher tariff costs onto shoppers in 2025 and then blamed a sales slowdown on those higher prices. The ultra-fast fashion retailer saw its year-over-year share of U.S. apparel, accessories and footwear card spending decrease in the fourth quarter of 2025 and first two quarters of 2026, according to Consumer Edge.

Shein did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Though Zara fell in AlixPartners' Consumer Sentiment Index, it has continued to grow sales and profits year over year. Even so, Lapinsky said worsening consumer views are something to watch, even if the company is doing well.

"What we found over the years with this consumer sentiment index is that sometimes it will be a bit of a leading indicator of what's to come," said AlixPartners' Lapinsky. "I'm not saying that this is going to be true for Zara, but I think that's one thing that we'll be watching as we look in the upcoming quarters and into 2027."

#94. Ex-GM CEO on Mary Barra succession planning: 'It's going to be tough'

Former General Motors CEO and Chairman Dan Akerson doesn't mince words when asked about the challenges the automaker faces in finding a successor for GM CEO Mary Barra: "It's going to be tough."

He should know. The 78-year-old businessman and U.S. Navy veteran helped with his own succession planning and recommended to GM's board in 2013 that Barra replace him as CEO. She has held the top job since Jan. 15, 2014.

Akerson said Barra will be hard to replace, citing her success and prominence as the first female CEO of any major automaker and the ongoing challenges facing the automotive industry, such as artificial intelligence, vehicle digitalization, and the rise of China and autonomous vehicles.

"It's going to be very, very dynamic," Akerson told CNBC during a phone interview. "It's going to be a big change."

Akerson declined to discuss potential internal candidates such as GM Chief Product Officer Sterling Anderson, CFO Paul Jacobson or President Mark Reuss, but he said the eventual successor needs to be dynamic and, if from the outside, "adaptable."

His comments to CNBC follow a Wall Street Journal report on Tuesday that GM is working with an executive search firm to identify possible outside candidates to succeed Barra, in addition to internal prospects. GM declined to comment on the report or Barra's succession.

Akerson, who came to GM as an outsider from The Carlyle Group in 2010, said selecting an outside candidate might be risky if there are already executives within the organization who have similar leadership and technical expertise.

"Is there anybody in the organization that has that background that you know better day to day over a period of time? You may be taking a risk by bringing in an outsider," he said.

The prospect of an outsider leading GM would be unusual in the auto industry, where major automakers have generally promoted internal CEO candidates, apart from periods of exceptional disruption such as the Great Recession.

Stellantis, BMW, Toyota Motor, Nissan Motor and Hyundai Motor have all appointed internal executives to their CEO positions in recent years.

Barra has said several times that her successor and her longevity in the role would be determined by the board.

A notable influence is likely Hewlett Packard veteran Patricia Russo, GM's longest-serving board member who has been on the board since 2009 and leads its CEO succession planning as independent lead director.

GM's CEO succession planning is done "at least annually," according to the company's yearly proxy filing. That process includes Barra providing the board "with recommendations for, and evaluations of, potential CEO successors and discusses developmental plans for these successors," according to the filing.

That is followed by board members engaging "with CEO candidates and senior management talent at Board and committee meetings and other forums to enable directors to personally assess candidates."

When Akerson was preparing to retire, Barra was among a pool of four candidates — two longtime GM employees and two relative newcomers — who were finalists for CEO in 2013, he said.

Akerson said he put the finalists through an extended evaluation, asking how they viewed GM's strengths and weaknesses, how they would organize the company, and their willingness to work together, potentially with other CEO candidates who didn't get the job.

Akerson said the final decision to appoint Barra was done at a day-and-a-half-long board meeting in Washington. The candidate field was narrowed to Barra and former GM executive Dan Ammann, an ex-Wall Street banker who was GM's CFO before leaving the automaker's Cruise unit, reportedly over a disagreement in strategy with Barra, in 2021.

"Mary's very deserving of all the accolades she's received," Akerson said. "She's done a terrific job."

Barra has been leading the automaker for nearly 13 years, which makes her the longest-serving CEO of the automaker since Alfred Sloan retired after a 23-year career as CEO in 1946.

Akerson said he believes that, like he did, Barra will have major influence in picking her successor. He described that dynamic as integral to continuing her legacy leading the automaker.

"This is important. I think if she doesn't pick the right person, in four or five years from now and things are back in the trough, that will tarnish her history. But I think she'll pick the right one," he said.

#91. A massive bearish trade just happened in the chip stocks

So much for buying the dip.As the S&P 500 entered correction territory and the Nasdaq-100 flirted with bear-market territory, down 19% from highs as of midday Friday, the single biggest options trade of the day was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) has a long way more to fall.Around 12:30 E.T., someone spent $93 million buying 20,000 380-strike puts expiring Sep. 17, 2027, according to trading data reviewed by SpotGamma and Volland, options-analytics platforms that both show the trade as a likely opening position, with volume exceeding existing open interest in the contract. The trade bets that the index will fall below $380, a nearly 8% decline from its current level. The SMH is off 40% from its recent all-time highs.

"That trade stands out big-time," wrote Jason DeLorenzo, founder and owner of Volland. "It is either a hedge or speculative, but either way it isn't a vote of confidence."The puts were bought for $46.50 a piece, an arguably expensive choice for a downside bet with volatility in the ETF trading at 70.

"You're paying a metric ton of Vega for that thing," added SpotGamma's Brent Kochuba, referring to the contract's sensitivity to changes in volatility of the underlying chip ETF. "Maybe they were a forced buyer, or maybe they're actually selling this, which can still be the case even though the price shows it happening closer to the ask."

The semiconductor index headed for a 9% drop this week alone.

#87. The bull market has a new problem. The 'smart money' is selling

Companies have shifted from buying back shares to issuing them, adding to the supply that the four-year-old bull market must absorb.

"No matter how you measure it, U.S. corporations have changed their behavior over the past two years, switching from repurchasing in 2024 to issuing today," Acadian Asset Management portfolio manager Owen Lamont wrote in a letter to investors this month. "The question you should be asking is: Why is the smart money selling?"

As of late last week, net new issuance of shares equaled 3.1% of total market capitalization, according to a recent Acadian analysis. While net issuance remains below the peaks during the tech bubble and the covid bubble, the analysis shows that the era of repurchases has ended and an era of equity issuance has begun.

And more supply came to the market this week with Anthropic's blockbuster IPO. The offering drew strong enthusiasm initially with the frontier AI laboratory selling shares in its IPO at $83 each, above the expected range and raising $81 billion. But the stock is falling on Friday and taking the broader stock market with it.

Some investors for years have cited the shrinking stock market as a reason why share prices can keep increasing. As companies repurchased stock, it shrunk the number of shares available for trading and so it helped boost prices. There was more money chasing fewer shares. Not to mention, it had the effect of boosting earnings per share by lowering the denominator in that ratio. Now that trend has reversed with increased supply acting as a potential headwind on the bull market.

A 2000 paper by Malcolm Baker and Jeffrey Wurgler found that when companies issued more equity over debt, low market returns followed, suggesting that managers timed the market when issuing securities. The Baker-Wurgler equity share, which measures equity issuance as a share of total stock and bond issuance, peaked at about 18% in 2021. It has risen in recent months but remains below that peak, Lamont wrote.

The Federal Reserve data show a similar trend in the year-over-year change in trailing 12-month gross stock and bond issuance. Debt gross issuance rose first in 2020, followed by equity issuance in 2021. More recently, debt issuance began climbing in 2024, followed by a surge in equity issuance in 2026.

Recent deals show companies tapping equity markets in different ways.

Oracle filed to sell $20 billion in stock through an at-the-market program and issued another $10 billion in common shares in November. In October, Meta priced a $25 billion common-stock offering and $12.5 billion in mandatory convertible preferred shares. Alphabet announced an $84.8 billion equity raise in June to help fund its AI infrastructure buildout. SpaceX raised $85.7 billion in its June IPO after underwriters exercised their overallotment.

"Before 2026, firms were net repurchasers of equity, consistent with the idea that they viewed their shares as undervalued. Today, for whatever reason, firms are issuing equity on net," Lamont wrote.

The investor said he cannot rule out that we are in an AI bubble now.

#82. Nvidia's 7-week losing streak is longest since 2008 financial crisis

The last time Wall Street was this down on Nvidia the chipmaker's flagship product was the GeForce graphics processor for high-end PC video games, and markets were reeling from the global financial crisis.

Nvidia shares fell nearly 10% on Friday, sliding for a seventh straight day as investors grew increasingly concerned about life after co-founder Jensen Huang. The stock is poised to close lower for a seventh consecutive week, the longest such stretch since 2008.

Shares of Nvidia are down 44% over the period, pushing the company's market cap down by $2.5 trillion. The stock is now trading at around 9.3 times forward earnings, while Advanced Micro Devices is valued at 29 times profit for the next 12 months.

Once the most valuable company, now, at a market cap of $3.2 trillion, Nvidia is fourth behind Apple and Microsoft and Alphabet.

Nvidia is reeling from the removal of Huang, who resigned on Nov. 12, after multiple reported affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who was allegedly a spy.

Harvey Jones, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, said earlier this week that Huang had resigned after "disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards and cultural norms."

But the chipmaker's losing streak started before Nvidia was thrown into crisis as investors worried over the sustainability of the AI buildout, particularly after the midterm elections, and emerging competition in AI chips from Google and AMD.

Even a better-than-expected earnings report, which included an increase to fiscal 2028 guidance, didn't stem the selling. Interim CEO Colette Kress, the company's longtime CFO, projected revenue growth next fiscal year of 75%, and said that customers aren't going anywhere.

On Tuesday's earnings call with analysts, Kress signed off by saying, "we've got this." In a company meeting the next day, executives told staff that it was moving its yearly review process, which can come with more stock, from March to December.

For investors, one major question is whether Nvidia can continue to innovate and sustain its commanding lead in the AI chip market without its visionary leader. Almost the entire company now is built around the AI trade, a phenomenon that began taking hold just four years ago with the launch of OpenAI's ChatGPT.

Before that, Nvidia's primary business was selling graphics cards used for video games. In mid-2008, the last time the stock was this deep into a weekly losing streak, Nvidia was generating less than $1 billion per quarter. Now it pulls in more than $1 billion per day. At the time, the entire market was in freefall due to the worst financial crisis since the Great Depression.

#77. Trump vows to stop U.S. companies from building data centers abroad, threatens 'tremendous tax'

President Donald Trump on Friday said U.S. companies will be banned from building data centers in other countries and will face a "tremendous tax" penalty if they do so.

"We're going to fight very hard for the data centers," Trump said at a White House press conference before announcing the new policy — which apparently came as news to his own staff.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story," Trump said. "I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now they're here."

Trump's latest push for more data-center construction in the U.S. came despite Americans repeatedly stating — and showing with their votes — that they dislike the structures.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey found that 64% of registered voters opposed an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% of respondents said they would support one.

If Trump follows through on his threat to clamp down on overseas data center construction, he will have to contend with the fact that numerous U.S. tech giants — including Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Meta and Oracle — have already built them in other countries.

International expansion represents a growth opportunity for the top cloud infrastructure providers, which want to serve clients from data centers that are nearby, sometimes to meet regulatory requirements.

Trump said Friday, however, that if companies want to continue building the centers outside the U.S., "they can, but we're going to put a tremendous tax on them."

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy, and they have everything. They're tremendous assets," Trump said.

The remarks came a day after Trump met with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin in China for a trilateral summit that appeared to produce few concrete deliverables on an array of pressing global issues.

Xi "congratulated me on how well we're doing" in artificial intelligence, Trump said, using his preferred term "super intelligence," which he said the Chinese president also likes better.

This is breaking news. Please refresh for updates.

— CNBC's Jordan Novet contributed reporting.

#73. BI Survey: Budgetary Constraints for 2027 Travel

Jody Lurie, Bloomberg Intelligence Credit Analyst, discussed her recent Bloomberg Survey covering budgetary constraints for 2027 travel. Lurie spoke with Bloomberg's Paul Sweeney and Scarlet Fu.on her recent BI surveys. US vacationers appear poised to spend more in 2027, but inflation is reshaping rather than curbing demand as higher core trip costs squeeze spending on extras, based on Bloomberg Intelligence's seventh US travel survey conducted Oct. 26-Nov. 4. About 71% believe they'll spend more, but only 45% would absorb higher-than-expected costs vs. 50% two years ago, while 53% cite inflation as a reason for bigger budgets. This shift favors midtier hotels and pressures airline and cruise upselling, while generative AI is a nearer-term threat to deal-hunting than booking for Expedia, Booking Holdings and Airbnb. (Source: Bloomberg)
